In four-strategy systems, increasing coalition closeness (alpha) generally increases cooperation, but can also lead to non-monotonic effects where too strong coalitions reduce cooperation due to the breakdown of indirect territorial competition. The mechanism acts as a wealth redistribution system, strengthening cooperators at the boundary of clusters but sometimes making them vulnerable to invasion by other types. The model does not include punishment or reward, but the coalition mechanism is compared to these in the discussion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency in PGGs, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides informative theoretical evidence about how payoff-redistribution mechanisms (such as coalitions or risk-sharing) can affect group efficiency and cooperation in spatial PGGs. The findings suggest that mechanisms which redistribute payoffs among players can have complex, sometimes non-monotonic effects on efficiency, depending on the structure of the game (e.g., synergy coefficient, spatial structure, coalition closeness). For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effect of any payoff-redistribution mechanism (including punishment, if it were present) may depend strongly on the details of the mechanism and the spatial or network structure. However, direct quantitative prediction of punishment effects is not possible from this paper.

- source: Khatun, K., Shen, C., & Tanimoto, J. (2025). Optional participation only provides a narrow scope for sustaining cooperation. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with optional participation, cooperation can only be sustained (and thus group efficiency improved) when nonparticipants have positive outside payoffs and do not harm the public good. The classic 'loner' model is a special case of this. If nonparticipants harm the public good or have negative outside payoffs, cooperation collapses and efficiency is low. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and thus does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings highlight the limitations of bottom-up, voluntary participation schemes for sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. However, it is highly informative about the baseline efficiency and cooperation levels in optional participation (loner) variants of the public goods game, which are sometimes considered as alternatives to punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper can inform the expected efficiency in control conditions where only optional participation is available, but not the effect of enabling punishment. It also clarifies that optional participation alone only supports cooperation (and thus higher efficiency) under narrow conditions.

- source: Dong, Y. T., He, Z. X., Shen, C., Shi, L., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Constructive agents nullify the ability of destructive agents to foster cooperation in public goods games. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game, destructive agents (who harm others at a cost to themselves) can induce cyclic dominance among strategies (cooperation, defection, destruction), which can sustain cooperation and thus increase group efficiency relative to the baseline. However, when constructive agents (who benefit others at a cost to themselves) are introduced, they do not by themselves promote cooperation or efficiency. If both types are present, constructive agents can nullify the ability of destructive agents to sustain cooperation if their benefit exceeds the harm from destructive agents. The efficiency outcome thus depends on the relative magnitude of constructive and destructive effects. The paper provides explicit replicator dynamics and invasion analyses to support these findings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with additional positive or negative externalities (constructive or destructive agents), but only indirectly relevant for classic punishment-based games. The 'destructive agent' is adjacent to punishment: it is a non-participant who harms others, but not a targeted, strategic punishment mechanism. The efficiency effects of destructive agents are positive (via cyclic dominance) when their harm is moderate, but are nullified if constructive agents' benefits are larger. The findings suggest that the presence and magnitude of positive and negative externalities can strongly moderate the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms. However, because the model is theoretical and does not include classic peer punishment (where players pay to reduce the payoff of specific others in response to their actions), direct quantitative predictions for standard punishment treatments should be made with caution. The results are most informative for settings where punishment is implemented as a non-strategic, non-targeted externality.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Quan, J., Ma, S. T., & Zhao, J. H. (2025). Evolutionary dynamics of spatial public goods games with insurance mechanisms. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that insurance mechanisms in spatial PGGs can both promote and inhibit cooperation and group efficiency, depending on the synergy coefficient, insurance premium, coverage, and the structure of the population. At low synergy coefficients, insurance (especially when purchased by defectors) can sustain cooperation through self-organized cyclic states, increasing group efficiency relative to baseline defection. However, at high synergy coefficients or when insured cooperators are prevalent, insurance can hinder cooperation due to indirect territorial competition. When insurers are allowed to adapt their strategies (two-layer network), the cooperation-promoting effect of insurance is often reduced, as insurers may refuse to transact with defectors, limiting the spread of insurance and altering the evolutionary dynamics. The model provides detailed phase diagrams mapping these effects, with efficiency interpreted as the prevalence of cooperative strategies and the resulting group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence on how insurance-like risk management mechanisms (adjacent to punishment) affect efficiency in spatial PGGs. While not a direct study of punishment, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency in games with sanctioning or risk-mitigation features. The results suggest that the effect of such mechanisms on efficiency is highly sensitive to game parameters (e.g., synergy coefficient, insurance cost/benefit, network structure) and the ability of third parties (insurers) to adapt. For prediction tasks, the paper highlights the importance of considering not just the presence of a sanctioning or insurance mechanism, but also who can access it, its cost-effectiveness, and the possibility of coevolution with external agents. The evidence is most directly applicable to spatial, structured populations with risk-mitigation options, and less so to standard punishment in classic PGGs.

- source: Kang, H. W., Li, Z. K., Shen, Y., Huang, Z. Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2024). From eligibility to suitability: Regulation and restriction of reputation-based access system on free-riding behavior in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that reputation-based exclusion mechanisms, which filter out low-reputation (likely free-riding) individuals before the PGG round, can significantly increase cooperation and total system payoff in spatial public goods games. The homogeneous exclusion model requires higher exclusion probability to match the performance of traditional (post-game) exclusion, but both converge to high cooperation and payoff at high exclusion probability. The heterogeneous exclusion model, which tailors exclusion probability to the reputation gap, outperforms both the homogeneous and traditional models in promoting cooperation and system payoff at high exclusion intensity. Both reputation-based models are especially effective at suppressing second-order free-riding, a limitation of traditional exclusion. The models are simulated on a 100x100 lattice with three strategies (C, D, EC), and results are robust across a range of synergy factors and exclusion costs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that pre-game, reputation-based exclusion mechanisms (a form of access control) can increase group payoff and cooperation in spatial PGGs, especially when exclusion is sufficiently strict. The findings suggest that, for games with spatial structure and reputation tracking, enabling such exclusion mechanisms is likely to increase efficiency relative to control (no exclusion or punishment). The effect is stronger for the heterogeneous (adaptive) exclusion model. The results are most directly applicable to spatial PGGs with continuous contribution, reputation tracking, and exclusion as the main sanctioning mechanism. The paper does not study classic costly punishment (where players pay to reduce others' payoffs post-game), so direct transfer to standard punishment-enabled PGGs should be cautious. However, the results are highly informative for predicting the effect of exclusion-based access control on efficiency, especially in spatial/reputation contexts.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2019). Cleverly handling the donation information can promote cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: Publishing the donation list partially (i.e., only for those who contribute above a threshold) increases total contributions to the public good compared to publishing all or none of the list. The optimal threshold for publication can be set dynamically as the mean of recent contributions, which is both effective and convenient. The model shows that this approach increases the sum of contributions and reduces the variance in contributions, indicating more uniform and higher cooperation. The effect is robust to changes in network connectivity and the window of historical data used. Gossip and self-assessment of face act as indirect social sanctions, motivating higher contributions. There is no explicit punishment or reward mechanism with costs and impact parameters as in standard PGG punishment treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that indirect social sanctions (via partial publication of contribution information and gossip) can increase total group contributions in a PGG-like environment. While it does not study explicit costly punishment, it suggests that reputational mechanisms can substitute for or complement formal punishment in promoting cooperation and group welfare. For prediction tasks, the findings imply that introducing a reputational/gossip-based sanction (by publicizing contributions above a threshold) can increase group payoff, especially if the threshold is set adaptively. However, the absence of explicit punishment cost/impact parameters means the results are not directly transferable to standard punishment treatments. The sum of contributions is a close but not exact proxy for efficiency.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2024). Evolutionary Dynamics of Preguidance Strategies in Population Games. *Ieee Transactions on Computational Social Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper introduces and analyzes two forms of preemptive guidance strategies (peer and pool guidance) in a one-shot public goods game with a pregame negotiation phase. Both forms of guidance can reduce the prevalence of defectors and second-order free riders (cooperators who do not guide). Pool guidance is more effective than peer guidance, especially at higher guidance costs. The main reported outcomes are the steady-state frequencies of each strategy, not group efficiency or payoff. The model does not include standard punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on group payoff or efficiency as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation evidence that preemptive guidance strategies can reduce free riding and increase the prevalence of guidance strategies in PGGs, especially when guidance costs are low. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in PGG-like environments. The guidance strategies studied are adjacent to, but not the same as, standard punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. The findings may be useful for understanding the dynamics of pre-commitment or preemptive intervention, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency outcomes from punishment in standard PGGs.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Chen, X. J. (2017). Alliance formation with exclusion in the spatial public goods game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in spatial public goods games, the introduction of an excluder strategy (which monitors and excludes defectors at a cost) can lead to the elimination of defectors when excluders form alliances with other cooperative strategies (cooperators or punishers). This effect is robust even when the cost of exclusion is extremely high, provided the population structure allows for the formation of sufficiently large clusters of excluders and cooperators/punishers. The main outcomes are the extinction probabilities of defectors and the conditions for the survival of excluders, not direct measures of group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that exclusion-based sanctions (a form of punishment) can be highly effective in structured populations, especially when combined with other cooperative strategies. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Instead, it suggests that under certain spatial and strategic conditions, sanctioning mechanisms can eliminate defection, which would likely increase efficiency, but this is not quantified. The findings are most relevant for understanding the qualitative impact of exclusion and alliances in spatially structured games, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Xiao, J. F., Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2023). Evolution of cooperation driven by sampling reward. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the sampling reward mechanism can effectively increase the average cooperation level in both PGG and CRD models. Higher reward thresholds and higher reward intensities promote cooperation, but also increase the cost to the institution. The effect of reward intensity is non-linear: above a critical threshold, further increases yield little additional cooperation. Sample size has a non-monotonic effect on cost, with intermediate sizes minimizing cost. The findings are robust across both game types. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that reward mechanisms (specifically, sampling reward) can increase cooperation rates in PGG-like environments, with effects moderated by reward threshold, intensity, and sample size. However, it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The paper is most useful for understanding how reward design parameters affect cooperation rates and institutional costs, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled. It does not provide empirical or theoretical results on punishment, nor does it analyze efficiency as defined by group payoff relative to full cooperation.

- source: Zhou, T. W., Ding, S., Fan, W. J., & Wang, H. (2016). An improved public goods game model with reputation effect on the spatial lattices. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that embedding a reputation effect into the utility function of agents in a spatial PGG model leads to a substantial increase in the stationary fraction of cooperators, especially as the reputation factor increases. The critical synergy factor required for cooperation to emerge is reduced, and the region of parameter space supporting full cooperation is expanded. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are based on Monte Carlo simulations and are relevant for understanding how reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation, but do not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation in spatial PGGs, but does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. For the downstream prediction task—predicting efficiency effects of punishment—this paper is only weakly informative. It suggests that non-monetary social mechanisms (like reputation) can increase cooperation, but does not provide data or analysis on how punishment affects efficiency, nor does it report group payoff or welfare. The game design dimensions are described in detail for the reputation mechanism, but not for punishment or efficiency.

- source: Zheng, J. J., Ren, T. Y., Ma, G., & Dong, J. H. (2021). The emergence and implementation of pool exclusion in spatial public goods game with heterogeneous ability-to-pay. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing pool exclusion, where excluders pay a cost to exclude defectors from the public good, can significantly increase the level of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. The effectiveness of exclusion depends on parameters such as the enhancement factor (r), the probability and effectiveness of exclusion, and the contribution rate to the exclusion pool. Cooperation is more likely to prevail when exclusion is effective and not overinvested. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators and excluders, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that exclusionary sanctions (pool exclusion) can increase cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially under certain parameter regimes. However, because the main outcome is cooperation level (not efficiency or group payoff), and because the sanction is exclusion rather than classic costly punishment, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs. The results suggest that effective, not overly costly exclusion can help sustain cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2017). Publishing the donation list incompletely promotes the emergence of cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: Publishing the donation list incompletely (only for those who contribute above a threshold) leads to higher total contributions and more equal contributions among players in a public goods game on a network. There is an optimal threshold for publication that maximizes these effects. The per capita number of neighbors and the amount of historical data players consider also moderate the effect, but the main result is robust: partial transparency outperforms full transparency or secrecy. The model is agent-based and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on social reputation and face loss as motivators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that social information mechanisms (such as partial publication of contribution lists) can increase cooperation in public goods games, but it does not study monetary punishment or its effect on efficiency. The findings are relevant for predicting the effect of information disclosure or reputation-based interventions, not for predicting the effect of adding costly punishment. The sum of contributions is a proxy for group welfare, but efficiency (as defined by payoff relative to full cooperation) is not directly reported. Use this paper as informative for the effect of social/reputational incentives, not for monetary punishment treatments.

- source: Yang, Z. H., Li, Z., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2013). Effects of adaptive dynamical linking in networked games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that adaptive dynamical linking—where agents lengthen or shorten social ties based on whether their partners cooperate or defect—can dramatically increase the prevalence of cooperation in both the prisoner's dilemma and public goods game. The effect is stronger when agents adjust their links more rapidly (higher adjustment rate, Δ), and under weak selection. The mechanism does not involve any explicit punishment, reward, or reputation system. The outcome measured is the frequency of full cooperation (all-cooperator absorbing state) in repeated simulations. There is no direct measurement or discussion of group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task of how punishment affects efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant as a demonstration that network adaptation (partner switching) can promote cooperation, but it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be contextually useful for understanding alternative mechanisms (partner choice) that can promote cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Hu, D., Liu, J. Z., Liu, C., & Chu, C. (2025). Dynamic incentives and environmental feedback in public goods games: Promoting cooperation through critical thresholds. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing dynamic, group-level environmental feedback (bonuses or penalties based on collective contribution thresholds) in a spatial public goods game strongly promotes cooperation, especially when the synergy factor is low. The effect is not due to individual punishment or reward, but to a group-level adjustment of payoffs. The main reported outcomes are cooperation rates and the distribution of strategies and incentives. The model shows that higher expectation thresholds for group contributions can, counterintuitively, make cooperation more likely, and that the system transitions from defection to cooperation as the dynamic incentive increases. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment mechanism. Instead, it models a group-level dynamic incentive (environmental feedback) that is not targeted at individuals and is not costly to assign. The findings may be indirectly relevant for understanding how group-level feedback mechanisms (distinct from punishment) can promote cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled versus disabled. The results are most relevant for adjacent mechanisms (dynamic group-level incentives) rather than direct punishment or sanctions.

- source: Sun, X. P., Zhu, H. R., Kang, H. W., Bi, Y. Z., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2025). The impact of memory reputation-induced tax and reward allocation on spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing a reputation-driven mechanism for adjusting local synergy factors in a spatial public goods game can significantly promote cooperation. The effect is strongest when the maximum possible synergy factor is high, the minimum is low, the adjustment step size is small, and the reputation threshold is moderate. Both excessively high and low reputation thresholds hinder cooperation. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward actions; instead, reputation indirectly affects payoffs by modulating the local multiplier. The main reported outcome is the proportion of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of explicit punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment mechanism. However, it is relevant for understanding how endogenous, reputation-driven changes to the payoff structure (via the synergy factor) can affect cooperation rates. The findings suggest that indirect, reputation-based incentives can substitute for or complement explicit punishment/reward mechanisms in promoting cooperation, but the results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides only adjacent, indirect evidence.

- source: Wang, M. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2020). The interplay of behaviors and attitudes in public goods game considering environmental investment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The introduction of an alliance strategy, where players pay an aid fee to help exploited allies, leads to a dramatic increase in the prevalence of cooperative behavior (C + A) in spatial PGGs. Alliance clusters are robust to defector invasion and can expand to dominate the population, especially for intermediate values of the synergy factor (r) and aid intensity (beta). The cooperation level is a non-monotonic function of both r and beta, with optimal intervals for each. Average payoffs per strategy are reported, showing that alliance players eventually outperform defectors and cooperators. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total welfare as a function of the alliance mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms analogous to mutual aid or alliance (distinct from punishment) can promote cooperation and increase the prevalence of cooperative strategies in spatial PGGs. However, because the model does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report group efficiency or total welfare, it is only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency effects of punishment. The findings suggest that payoff redistribution among cooperators can be highly effective in suppressing defection, but the results cannot be directly transferred to settings with explicit punishment. The paper is most useful for understanding the effects of mutual aid or alliance-type mechanisms, not punishment.

- source: Wang, W. X., Lai, Y. C., & Armbruster, D. (2011). Cascading failures and the emergence of cooperation in evolutionary-game based models of social and economical networks. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in evolutionary games on networks (including PGG), introducing a survival threshold (death/bankruptcy) leads to catastrophic cascades when defection is present. After such cascades, only cooperators survive, regardless of network topology or game type. Defectors may gain higher payoffs temporarily but are ultimately eliminated. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcomes are the fraction of dead agents, the survival of cooperators, and the conditions for extinction or survival, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. The model's 'sanction' is the endogenous removal of agents with low payoffs, not a punishment technology as in standard PGG experiments. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency. It may provide some adjacent theoretical context about the consequences of defection and the importance of cooperation for system survival, but not about the efficiency impact of punishment interventions.

- source: Quan, J., Yang, W. J., Li, X., Wang, X. J., & Yang, J. B. (2020). Social exclusion with dynamic cost on the evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing reputation-dependent imitation and a strict reputation evaluation mechanism (low tolerance for defection) in a spatial public goods game leads to higher and more stable levels of cooperation (as measured by the frequency of cooperators). The effect is stronger when the enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR) is higher. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The results are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes. Its findings about reputation-dependent imitation and tolerance in reputation evaluation may be contextually informative for understanding mechanisms that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The absence of payoff-based outcomes and the lack of a punishment mechanism limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Yang, R. (2019). Using rewards reasonably: The effects of stratified-rewards in public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that stratified-rewards can promote, maintain, or even inhibit efficiency (measured as available funds for public goods) depending on the scope and distribution of rewards. If the number of people who receive rewards is small and the number who get high-level rewards is controlled, available funds can increase. If too many people get high rewards, available funds can fall below the baseline, even if all players cooperate. The sum of contributions and the standard deviation of contributions are also affected by reward parameters. The fraction of cooperators is always high with rewards, but this does not guarantee high efficiency. The optimal reward design balances the number of recipients and the cost of rewards to maximize available funds.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of reward mechanisms on efficiency in PGG-like environments is highly sensitive to the design of the reward scheme. It shows that simply enabling rewards does not guarantee higher efficiency; the number of rewarded players, the proportion of high-level rewards, and the cost of rewards all interact to determine the net group payoff. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of a reward mechanism can have positive, neutral, or negative effects on efficiency, depending on these design details. Since the paper does not study punishment, it does not directly inform the effect of punishment, but it does highlight the importance of cost structures and incentive distribution in predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., & Chen, Z. Q. (2016). The public goods game with a new form of shared reward. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a shared reward, proportional to the number of cooperators, can enable cooperation to invade and become stable in a population of defectors if the reward parameter exceeds a critical threshold. This threshold depends negatively on the population size and the public goods multiplier, and positively on the group size. Larger populations and higher public goods multipliers make it easier for cooperation to be favored, while larger group sizes make it harder. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, and thus does not directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. However, it does provide theoretical insight into how reward mechanisms (distinct from punishment) can affect the evolution of cooperation and group payoff. For the downstream prediction task focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but may be useful for understanding the comparative role of reward-based interventions.

- source: Hua, S. J., & Liu, L. J. (2024). Coevolutionary dynamics of population and institutional rewards in public goods games. *Expert Systems with Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with institutional rewards, if the reward strength is adaptively increased when cooperation is low and decreased when cooperation is high, the system can reach stable equilibria with sustained cooperation and group welfare. The model shows that: (1) a stable coexistence of cooperation and reward strength is possible; (2) full cooperation can be achieved with minimal reward intensity if the per capita incentive is above a threshold; (3) moderate cooperation can be sustained with either low or high reward intensity, depending on parameter values; (4) in some cases, even high reward intensity cannot prevent full defection if incentives are too low. The model provides explicit conditions for these outcomes in terms of group size, marginal per-capita return, and reward parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with institutional rewards, but not for games with punishment. It provides theoretical predictions for how adaptive reward mechanisms affect group payoff and cooperation, given game parameters such as group size, marginal per-capita return, and reward strength. However, it does not address punishment, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are useful for understanding the efficiency impact of reward-based interventions, especially when reward strength is allowed to adapt to cooperation levels.

- source: Wang, J. W., Yu, F. Y., He, J. L., Chen, W., Xu, W. S., Dai, W. H., & Ming, Y. X. (2023). Promotion, Disintegration and Remediation of group cooperation under heterogeneous distribution system based on peer rating. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: Redistribution rules that reward willingness to cooperate (high alpha) and are neutral with respect to initial endowment (beta near zero) lead to the highest group efficiency (average payoff) and lowest inequality. Redistribution that favors the poor (negative beta) or the rich (positive beta) increases inequality and does not maximize group payoff. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning; all effects are due to redistribution of payoffs after the public goods game. The findings challenge the assumption that subsidizing the poor in such games always promotes cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it is highly informative about how redistribution mechanisms (distinct from punishment) affect efficiency and cooperation in heterogeneous populations. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it does provide a useful contrast: redistribution based on willingness to cooperate can increase efficiency, while redistribution based on endowment does not. The game design dimensions are well specified for redistribution, but not for punishment.

- source: Zhao, L., Ye, X. J., Huang, Z. G., Sun, J. T., Yang, L., Do, Y., & Wang, Y. H. (2010). Promotion of cooperation in the form C0C1D classified by 'degree grads' in a scale-free network. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in scale-free networks, cooperation can persist even when the PGG enhancement factor r < 1, due to a mechanism where high-degree ('hub') cooperators (C0) are imitated by their neighbors (C1), who may receive negative payoffs but still support the hub. The average payoff of agents is analyzed as a function of their network position (degree grads), showing that only the hubs (C0) receive positive payoffs, while most other cooperators (C1) have negative payoffs. The system-level average payoff increases with r, but the persistence of cooperation at low r is due to network structure and imitation, not payoff incentives. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is useful for understanding how network structure and imitation can sustain cooperation and affect payoff distribution, but it does not inform predictions about the impact of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task (treatment efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is not directly relevant, as punishment is not present or discussed as a mechanism.

- source: Wang, C. Q., Lin, Z. Z., & Rothman, D. S. (2022). Public goods game on coevolving networks driven by the similarity and difference of payoff. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the way agents update strategies—locally, globally, or a mix—has complex effects on cooperation and payoffs in spatial PGGs. When both cooperators and defectors have the same tendency for global updating, local updating is more beneficial for cooperation at low synergy factors (r), while both local and global updating can support cooperation at high r, but their combination can be detrimental. When cooperators and defectors differ in their global updating propensity, the system's cooperation and payoff outcomes depend sensitively on these propensities. Moderate global updating by defectors and low global updating by cooperators most favors cooperation and higher payoffs. The paper does not study punishment or its effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGGs. It is useful for understanding how information structure (local vs global updating) and network effects influence cooperation and payoffs, but does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled versus punishment-disabled efficiency. The absence of punishment mechanisms means it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Wang, J. W., Chen, W., Yu, F. Y., He, J. L., & Xu, W. S. (2022). Wealth-based rule favors cooperation in costly public goods games when individual selection is inevitable. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in both independent and interdependent PGGs with two cooperative strategies, the coexistence and prevalence of each strategy depend on the synergy factor and the difference in input costs. In the interdependent model, effective input and economic benefit are maximized when the two strategies are present in similar proportions. The interdependence mechanism (In-PGG) can sometimes lead to more quasi-cooperation than the independent mechanism (An-PGG), especially when the synergy factor and the difference in input costs are small. However, the paper does not directly report or quantify group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the stable proportions of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctions. It is useful for understanding how the structure of cooperation (independent vs interdependent strategies) and parameter settings affect the prevalence of cooperative behaviors, but it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes in a way that can be directly mapped to the prediction task. The findings may be indirectly relevant for understanding how structural features of the game (such as interdependence of contributions) affect the emergence of cooperation, but not for the effect of punishment.

- source: Wang, Q. S., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2025). Coevolutionary dynamics of feedback-evolving games in structured populations. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in a public goods game with environmental investment is promoted when social attention (driven by individual attitudes and environmental feedback) is high and when the investment budget exceeds a threshold. The model identifies three types of public response mechanisms to environmental degradation, with the 'precautionary' mechanism (rapid response to early degradation) being most beneficial for cooperation. The level of cooperation is sensitive to the parameters governing attention and forgetfulness, but the level of social attention itself is robust to these parameters within certain intervals. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present, and efficiency or group payoff is not analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its findings are limited to the dynamics of cooperation and social attention in a PGG with environmental investment and evolving attitudes. It may provide indirect context for how non-punitive mechanisms (like attention or investment) affect cooperation, but is not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Yang, Z. Q. L., Li, C. C., Wang, X., & Tian, Y. L. (2025). PPO-ACT: Proximal policy optimization with adversarial curriculum transfer for spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The PPO-ACT algorithm, which combines proximal policy optimization with adversarial curriculum transfer, enables higher and more robust cooperation rates in spatial public goods games compared to standard PPO, Q-learning, and Fermi update rules. PPO-ACT achieves stable all-cooperator states at lower enhancement factors (r) than other algorithms, and is robust to challenging initial conditions (e.g., all-defector starts). The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators; no explicit analysis of group efficiency or total payoff is provided. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how advanced learning algorithms affect cooperation rates in spatial PGGs, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The design dimensions and results may be useful for modeling baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of punishment, but not for the downstream task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Zhang, L., Zhang, L. M., & Huang, C. W. (2022). Defectors in bad circumstances possessing higher reputation can promote cooperation. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a reputation rule—where reputation is based on matching one's strategy to one's neighbors—combined with heterogeneous investment allocation (parameterized by α), strongly promotes cooperation in spatial PGGs. Higher heterogeneity (larger α) lowers the critical synergy factor required for cooperation to emerge and increases the region of parameter space where full cooperation is stable. The results are robust to changes in network structure (square lattice, Moore neighborhood, scale-free network), total investment, noise, and initial cooperation proportion. However, the study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGG-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how reputation and heterogeneous investment can promote cooperation, but not for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects. It may be contextually useful for understanding alternative cooperation-promoting mechanisms.

- source: Shi, Z. Y., Wei, W., Zheng, H. W., & Zheng, Z. M. (2023). Bidirectional supervision: An effective method to suppress corruption and defection under the third party punishment mechanism of donation games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when the weight of reputation in the strategy update rule is moderate, expanding the influence range of high-reputation individuals promotes cooperation (higher fraction of cooperators). When the reputation weight is low, expanding the influence range actually reduces cooperation. The effect of expanding influence range has diminishing returns (marginal effect). The model is a spatial PGG on a square lattice, with no punishment or reward mechanisms. Payoff is only discussed for subgroups and not as a main outcome. The findings are about cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are about the effect of reputation-based influence range on cooperation rates in a spatial PGG. While it provides insight into how information and influence structures can affect cooperation, it is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Wang, S., Li, Z., Li, W. D., & Jiang, H. (2022). Continuous Spatial Public Goods Game Based on Particle Swarm Optimization with Memory Stability. *Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in a continuous spatial PGG with PSO-based learning, the memory stability coefficient (α) has a complex effect on the average cooperation level. At low enhancement factors (r), high memory stability (large α) helps maintain a moderate level of cooperation, while reducing memory stability (lower α) leads to a collapse of cooperation. At high r, reducing memory stability (lower α) allows the system to forget low-investment strategies and promotes the formation of high-cooperation clusters, increasing the average cooperation level. The effect of memory stability is more pronounced when players rely more on their own history (high ω) than on neighbor imitation. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are included in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present. The findings are relevant for understanding how learning rules and memory affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The results may be indirectly useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in spatial, continuous PGGs with PSO-type learning, but cannot inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Tomassini, M., & Antonioni, A. (2021). Computational behavioral models in public goods games with migration between groups. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that allowing unsatisfied players to migrate between groups increases average contribution in public goods games, especially for lower enhancement factors (r). The effect of group size and update timing (synchronous vs asynchronous) is minor. Heterogeneity in migration propensity has little effect on average contribution compared to a homogeneous population. The amount of information available to players (own payoff/contribution vs also knowing the number of contributors in the group) does not substantially change the results. However, the paper does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on migration as an alternative to punishment may be contextually informative for understanding other forms of exit or mobility, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The reported outcomes are average contribution rates, not efficiency or payoff, and thus are only adjacent to the prediction task.

- source: Kang, H. W., Jiang, C., Shen, Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2025). Neighbor-aware reinforcement learning fosters cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that integrating neighbor-aware reinforcement learning (NI) and reputation mechanisms in a spatial public goods game model can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators and promote the formation of stable cooperator clusters. The effect is especially strong when agents can observe a wider neighborhood (larger perceptual range). Reputation alone can promote cooperation if it is sufficiently weighted in the reward function, but the combination of NI and reputation produces a synergistic effect. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms in the sense of costly peer or institutional sanctions/rewards. The main dependent variable is the cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how social learning, reputation, and local information can promote cooperation, but not for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment. The model and results may be useful for understanding moderators of cooperation in spatial and learning-based PGGs, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

  findings: The paper finds that longer memory (up to a threshold) and slower memory decay promote higher levels of cooperation in both scale-free and small-world networks. - source: Chen, Y. S., Yang, H. X., Guo, W. Z., & Liu, G. G. (2018). Promotion of cooperation based on swarm intelligence in spatial public goods games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The introduction of swarm intelligence (where players update their strategies based on both their own best past strategy and the best current strategy in their neighborhood) into a continuous spatial public goods game leads to a strong promotion of cooperation. The level of cooperation depends on the multiplication factor (r) and the weight given to personal vs. neighborhood best strategies (c1). For low c1 (neighborhood-focused), cooperation can dominate at high r, but is vulnerable at low r. For high c1 (personal best-focused), cooperation is more stable even at low r, but the system is more diverse in strategies. The main reported outcome is the average cooperation level, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how alternative strategy updating rules (swarm intelligence) affect cooperation rates, not payoffs or efficiency.   dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that allowing individuals to choose between multiple public goods games (resources) with different enhancement factors can promote cooperation. Defectors tend to cluster in the highest quality resource, while cooperators can 'shelter' in lower quality resources, avoiding exploitation. Cooperation is maximized when the two highest quality resources are similar in quality. The model is analyzed both analytically (replicator-mutator dynamics) and via simulation, showing robust results across parameter ranges. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present. The main outcome is the density of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding how choice between public resources affects cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model does not include punishment, reward, or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding the impact of resource choice and group structure on cooperation.

  findings: The model shows that cooperation (measured as the fraction of conditional cooperators) only survives in multicultural states with high cultural diversity and very low or zero noise. In monocultural states or with any significant noise, cooperation dies out. The number of participants in public goods games is inversely related to cultural diversity. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it does provide contextual evidence that group structure, diversity, and noise can strongly affect cooperation rates. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding the role of cultural diversity and conditional cooperation in the absence of punishment.

- source: Stock, E. V., Valverde, P., González-Avella, J. C., Iglesias, J. R., Gonçalves, S., & da Silva, R. (2025). Cyclical oscillations and absorbing-state probabilities in optional public goods games: Interplay of reward and group size. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the OPGG exhibits rich dynamical behavior, including cyclic dominance among strategies and multistability in absorbing states, depending on the multiplication factor (r) and group size (S). For r < 2, loners dominate; for r > S, cooperators dominate; and for 2 < r < S, the system can end in any of the three absorbing states, with the likelihood and duration of cycles depending on S and r. Larger group sizes and higher r values tend to favor cooperation, but the model does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide detailed evidence on how group size and the multiplication factor affect the stability and dynamics of cooperation, defection, and abstention in optional public goods games. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide useful context for understanding baseline dynamics in the absence of punishment.

  findings: The introduction of a dynamic scale return coefficient, governed by the difference between a group's average reputation and the population average, promotes the evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games. The effect is robust across lattice and small-world network structures. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

It is focused on a different cooperation-promoting mechanism (reputation-based environmental feedback) and does not include or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Uniform heterogeneity is most conducive to high and stable cooperation in most parameter ranges, but exponential heterogeneity can outperform in some cases. Stronger heterogeneity (higher amplitude) generally promotes higher cooperation. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcome, so the findings are about cooperation rates, not efficiency.

Its findings are limited to how heterogeneity in the scale return coefficient affects average cooperation (contribution) levels in a spatial, continuous-contribution PGG. It is not relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be contextually useful for understanding how nonlinearities and heterogeneity in returns affect cooperation rates.

- source: Xiao, Y., & Hua, D. Y. (2012). Promotion of Cooperation in a Spatial Public Goods Game with Long Range Learning and Mobility. *Chinese Physics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the evolution of cooperation in a spatial public goods game is strongly affected by the interplay of population density, learning range, and mobility range. When the learning range is larger than the mobility range, small local groups at low population density support high cooperation. At moderate densities, mobility allows cooperators to escape defector invasions, supporting stable cooperation. When the mobility range is larger than the learning range, high population density enables the formation of compact cooperator domains, which also promotes cooperation. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators; no efficiency or group payoff results are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. It is relevant for understanding how spatial structure, mobility, and learning range affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based efficiency outcomes or the effect of punishment. Therefore, it should not be weighted heavily in predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in the context of punishment.

  findings: The study finds that integrating reputation into both investment decisions and payoff calculations, especially when using double Q-learning, substantially increases the cooperation rate in spatial public goods games. The effect is strongest when the weight of reputation in payoff (η), the synergy factor (r), and the reputation sensitivity parameter (δ) are all sufficiently high. The model demonstrates that cooperation can be sustained even in challenging environments, but only when these parameters are jointly favorable. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rate, reputation distribution).

- source: Lv, S. J., Li, J. Y., & Zhao, C. H. (2025). Reinforcement learning in spatial public goods games with environmental feedbacks. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that both higher synergy coefficient (r) and stronger environmental feedback (alpha) promote higher levels of cooperation in the SPGG. The effect of the Q-learning discount factor (gamma) on cooperation is non-monotonic and depends on the values of r and alpha. When r or alpha is low, higher gamma (more future-oriented agents) promotes cooperation; when r and alpha are high, lower gamma (more myopic agents) is more favorable for cooperation. The results are based on agent-based simulations and focus on the fraction of cooperators and spatial clustering, not on payoff or efficiency outcomes. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant for understanding how environmental feedback and reinforcement learning affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based outcomes or for the effect of punishment. The game design dimensions covered (e.g., player count, mpcr, rounds, absence of punishment/reward) may be useful for contextualizing baseline cooperation in spatial PGGs with environmental feedback, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Wang, X. Y., He, Z. X., & Shi, L. (2024). Conformity poses a double-edged sword effect on the evolution of cooperation within heterogeneous endowment populations. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the existence and maintenance of pure cooperator groups in a multi-group public goods game can promote the rise of cooperation in the population, even without mechanisms like punishment or reward. However, mutation and migration allow defectors to invade cooperator groups, undermining cooperation. Group division (splitting of large groups) can counteract the negative effects of mutation by generating new cooperator groups, but only if mutation rates are not too high and group division is sufficiently frequent. The synergy factor (r) and contribution level (c) also moderate these dynamics. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are not directly transferable to payoff-based prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it provides indirect context on how group structure, mutation, and migration affect cooperation rates. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, except as a reminder that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., group division, migration) can strongly affect cooperation, and that the presence or absence of such mechanisms should be considered when interpreting or predicting the effects of punishment.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in public goods games on coevolving networks where agents disconnect from neighbors with similar payoff and connect to those with different payoff, the maintenance of cooperation depends on the balance between these two tendencies. Cooperation is best sustained when the rates of disconnection and connection are moderate and well-matched. Too much network time-dependence (frequent rewiring) generally undermines cooperation compared to the classic static network case. The emergence and stability of cooperation are associated with changes in network cohesion, but the paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how network coevolution (rewiring based on payoff similarity/difference) affects cooperation rates, not efficiency. It should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency.

  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneity in investment, where individuals allocate more to groups that have yielded higher payoffs in the recent past, significantly increases the fraction of cooperators in spatial PGGs. Shorter memory (small M) and lower weight on earlier payoffs (small or negative omega) further enhance cooperation. Stronger heterogeneity (higher alpha) also promotes cooperation. These effects are robust to changes in network topology (square lattice vs. ER random networks). However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes.

For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as providing only adjacent, not direct, evidence.

  findings: The study finds that both population density and agent mobility (free-floating probability) have non-monotonic effects on the frequency of cooperation in the OPGG.   findings: The paper uses agent-based simulations to study how conformity-driven behavior affects cooperation in public goods games with binary heterogeneous endowments (rich and poor). The main finding is a 'double-edged sword' effect: when the multiplication factor (synergy of cooperation) is high, conformity enhances cooperation by strengthening network reciprocity; when the multiplication factor is low, conformity inhibits cooperation, especially among the poor, by allowing defection to infiltrate cooperative clusters. These effects are robust to variations in conformity strength, the proportion of rich, and the extension to multiplayer PGGs. However, the study only reports on cooperation rates, not on efficiency or group payoff.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study demonstrates that in spatial PGGs with imitation dynamics, there is an optimal homogeneous contribution value that maximizes cooperation (measured as cooperator density) for a given lattice structure. Introducing heterogeneity in contributions (i.e., allowing cooperators to contribute different amounts) can reduce the overall level of cooperation compared to this optimum, due to parasitic interactions where higher-contribution cooperators are exploited by lower-contribution ones. The effect of heterogeneity depends on the lattice structure: on square lattices, heterogeneity is detrimental to cooperation at the optimum, while on triangular lattices (with clustering), the effect is less negative. The main dependent variable is the equilibrium density of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured.

Its findings are relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in contributions (a form of player heterogeneity) affects cooperation rates, but not efficiency or payoff outcomes. The results may be useful for contextualizing the impact of contribution heterogeneity as a moderator, but not for the core prediction task involving punishment. The absence of punishment means this paper should not be used as primary evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game model, moderate levels of habitat destruction increase the fraction of cooperators, while high levels cause cooperation to collapse. The multiplication factor (r) increases cooperation monotonically. The model does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators in the population.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff. It may provide some indirect context about how environmental stressors (like habitat destruction) affect cooperation rates, but it is not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Gao, B., Li, X., Hou, S. X., Jia, D. Y., & Du, M. J. (2019). Resolving public goods dilemma by giving the poor more support. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that narrowing the income gap among group members (negative alpha, i.e., redistributive policies favoring the poor) increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial public goods game, while increasing income inequality (positive alpha) reduces cooperation. The model is simulated on a lattice, and the main outcome is the proportion of cooperators under different redistribution regimes. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are present, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings about redistribution and cooperation rates may be of indirect interest for understanding how inequality and redistribution affect cooperation, but it is not relevant for the core prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Huang, K. K., Zheng, X. P., Yang, Y. Q., & Wang, T. (2015). Behavioral evolution in evacuation crowd based on heterogeneous rationality of small groups. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that increasing the degree of rationality (parameter alpha) in a subset of players leads to a higher fraction of cooperators in the population. When more players use rational allocation strategies, cooperation is promoted, and the phase transition points for cooperation occur at lower enhancement factors. The ratio of rational to conventional players also modulates cooperation: more rational players lead to more cooperation. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or direct efficiency/payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in rationality and investment strategies can promote cooperation, but it does not inform predictions about punishment-enabled versus punishment-disabled efficiency. The absence of punishment and the focus on cooperation rates rather than payoffs limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.
