- source: Chen, M. H., Wang, L., Wang, J., Sun, S. W., & Xia, C. Y. (2015). Impact of individual response strategy on the spatial public goods game within mobile agents. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the order in which agents update their strategy and move (strategy update order) can significantly affect the evolution of cooperation and social instability in spatial PGGs with mobile agents. Different aspiration evaluation methods (how agents assess satisfaction based on their own and neighbors' payoffs) have only minor effects on cooperation and agglomeration, but can influence social instability. The model does not include punishment or reward, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how mobility and aspiration-based strategy updating affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based efficiency outcomes or the impact of punishment. It may provide weak contextual information about the role of behavioral updating and mobility, but is not informative for the core prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Wang, X. J., & Lv, S. J. (2019). The roles of particle swarm intelligence in the prisoner's dilemma based on continuous and mixed strategy systems on scale-free networks. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The authors propose a method to predict the strategies of players whose current actions are hidden, based on the similarity of their past behavior to that of observed players. The method is tested on both the prisoner's dilemma and public goods games on a spatial lattice. The prediction method achieves high accuracy (success rate up to 0.9) in identifying hidden strategies, with performance depending on the length of historical data and game parameters (temptation to defect in PDG, enhancement factor in PGG). The paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or related environments. It does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. Its findings are not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: ABREU, D., MILGROM, P., & PEARCE, D. (1991). INFORMATION AND TIMING IN REPEATED PARTNERSHIPS. *Econometrica*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated partnership games with imperfect monitoring, the ability to sustain efficient cooperation through punishment is fundamentally limited by the informativeness of the monitoring technology and the timing of information. When monitoring is imperfect, reducing the interest rate (making players more patient) increases the efficiency of equilibrium outcomes, potentially allowing near-first-best payoffs. However, shortening the period of fixed action or reducing the lag with which information is reported can actually decrease efficiency, sometimes eliminating the possibility of cooperation altogether. Delaying information release can, counterintuitively, increase efficiency by reducing the cost of deterring deviations. The paper provides generalizations to n-player symmetric games and shows that these results are robust to more complex action and signal spaces.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency of repeated public-goods-like games with punishment under imperfect monitoring. It shows that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the informativeness of monitoring, the timing of information, and the patience of players. In particular, enabling punishment does not guarantee high efficiency unless monitoring is sufficiently informative and the timing of information and action periods is chosen carefully. For prediction, the paper suggests that increasing patience (lower interest rate) is reliably associated with higher efficiency, but increasing the frequency of action or information updates can reduce efficiency when monitoring is imperfect. The explicit formulas and comparative statics in the paper can be used to estimate efficiency given the relevant game design dimensions.

- source: Lu, Z. Y., Hua, S. J., Wang, L. C., & Liu, L. J. (2024). Hybrid reward-punishment in feedback-evolving game for common resource governance. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a feedback-evolving common resource game (a close variant of the public goods game), the introduction of punishment (or a hybrid of reward and punishment) by a third-party institution significantly increases the likelihood of achieving both full cooperation and sustainable resource levels, which are direct analogs to high efficiency. Pure reward is less effective than punishment or hybrid schemes, especially when the resource growth rate is moderate. Increasing the per capita incentive (tax) and allocating more of the incentive budget to punishment (lower w) both make the ideal outcome (full cooperation and resource sustainability) more likely. When the resource growth rate is too low, no incentive scheme is effective. The results are robust across a range of parameter values and are supported by both analytical and numerical phase diagrams.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in environments where resource renewal is not too slow, adding punishment (or a hybrid of reward and punishment) increases the likelihood of achieving high efficiency (full cooperation and resource sustainability) compared to reward alone or no incentives. The effect is stronger when the per capita incentive is higher and when more of the incentive budget is allocated to punishment. The model is deterministic and infinite-population, but the qualitative insights about the superiority of punishment (or hybrid) over pure reward for efficiency are likely to generalize to similar institutional public goods settings. The paper does not address stochasticity, finite groups, or experimental data, but its results are directly relevant for theoretical prediction.

- source: Wang, Y. F., Vasilakos, A. V., & Ma, J. H. (2015). VPEF: A Simple and Effective Incentive Mechanism in Community-Based Autonomous Networks. *Ieee Transactions on Network and Service Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: VPEF, an incentive mechanism based on voluntary participation, round-based entry fees, and voluntary costly punishment, leads to high efficiency in community-based collaborative networks. The system spends most of its time in the punisher state, where all participants contribute and punish defectors, minimizing efficiency loss. The efficiency loss is analytically shown to be 4/(8+M), which becomes negligible for large communities. Simulations confirm the theoretical predictions. The mechanism is robust to changes in group size, entry fee (as long as it is not too high), and multiplexing/diversity factor. The model assumes strong selection (users imitate higher-payoff strategies) and does not require tracking long-term behavior or permanent exclusion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling voluntary costly punishment in a public-goods-like environment with voluntary participation and a small entry fee can dramatically increase efficiency, driving the system to near-full cooperation (punisher state) and minimizing efficiency loss. The efficiency loss formula (4/(8+M)) can be used to predict the efficiency of the punishment-enabled game, given the number of users. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values, provided the multiplexing/diversity factor is above 1 and defectors can be reliably detected. The model is most applicable to dynamic, anonymous, and voluntary participation settings, and less so to settings with continuous contribution levels or where punishment/reward is not voluntary.

- source: Wang, L. C., Hua, S. J., Liu, Y. Y., Zhang, L., & Liu, L. J. (2024). Threshold incentive mechanisms for the sustainable management of public resources. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that threshold-based punishment and reward mechanisms can effectively promote cooperation and sustainable management of public resources, leading to higher efficiency (resource sustainability and group payoff) at lower cost than continuous incentives. Punishment is only triggered when the number of cooperators exceeds a threshold, targeting defectors, while reward is triggered when cooperation is low or resources are depleted. These mechanisms can stabilize the system at higher resource levels and higher cooperation rates, but their effectiveness is limited if the resource's natural growth rate is too low or the initial resource stock is too small. The models also show that increasing population size can reduce equilibrium resource levels, and that the interplay of reward/punishment thresholds and strengths is important for optimal outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that threshold-triggered punishment (and reward) mechanisms in public-goods-like resource games can increase efficiency relative to no incentives, especially when the resource can regenerate and initial conditions are not too dire. The effect of punishment is more positive for efficiency when it is targeted (threshold-based) rather than universal, and when combined with reward mechanisms. The model's explicit tracking of average payoffs and resource levels makes its results directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in games with similar design dimensions. However, the results are theoretical and may not capture all real-world behavioral nuances.

- source: Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2018). Punishment and inspection for governing the commons in a feedback-evolving game. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Punishment and inspection mechanisms can promote cooperation and prevent overexploitation in a common-pool resource game, but their ability to improve group efficiency (resource sustainability) depends critically on the intrinsic growth rate of the resource. If the resource renews too slowly, even perfect cooperation enforced by strong punishment leads to resource depletion and low efficiency. At moderate growth rates, strong punishment and inspection can achieve both high cooperation and sustainable resource levels, but weak institutions result in mixed strategy populations and depleted resources. At high growth rates, the resource is always sustainable regardless of punishment, and punishment only affects the fraction of cooperators versus defectors. Thus, the efficiency gain from punishment is context-dependent and can be absent or even negative if resource dynamics are unfavorable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like common-pool resource games is highly contingent on the ecological context, especially the resource's intrinsic growth rate. When predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, it is crucial to consider not only the game design dimensions (such as punishment cost and effectiveness) but also the underlying dynamics of the resource. In settings where the resource cannot recover quickly, punishment may not improve efficiency even if it increases cooperation. In more favorable ecological conditions, punishment can have a strong positive effect on efficiency, but only if it is sufficiently effective (high detection probability and/or fine). Thus, the paper suggests that the efficiency effect of punishment is not universal and must be interpreted in light of resource dynamics.

- source: Okada, I., Yamamoto, H., Toriumi, F., & Sasaki, T. (2015). The Effect of Incentives and Meta-incentives on the Evolution of Cooperation. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a theoretical evolutionary game framework, cooperation and high efficiency (group payoff) can only be stably achieved if the incentive system includes second-order incentives for first-order rewards. Systems with only first-order punishment or reward, or with second-order incentives for punishment but not for reward, cannot maintain cooperation or efficiency in the long run due to the second-order free-rider problem. The cost-effect ratio of incentives (how much impact per unit cost) is a critical parameter: higher ratios make it easier for cooperation to be sustained. The model predicts that systems with second-order rewards for first-order incentives are more robust and can achieve high efficiency with fewer incentive-providers than systems with second-order punishments. The analysis is entirely theoretical, using replicator dynamics, and does not provide empirical estimates of efficiency but gives qualitative and parametric conditions for when efficiency is high or low under different incentive structures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment and reward systems (including meta-incentives) on efficiency in public-goods-like games. It suggests that simply enabling punishment is not sufficient for high efficiency; the structure of incentives, especially the presence of second-order incentives for rewarding, is crucial. If a game design includes only first-order punishment, efficiency will not improve in the long run. If second-order incentives for first-order rewards are present and the cost-effect ratio is high, efficiency can be high. The model also clarifies that the effect of punishment on efficiency is conditional on the broader incentive structure. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that the presence or absence of second-order incentives, and the cost-effectiveness of punishment/reward, are key moderators of the efficiency effect of punishment.

- source: Wang, J. F., & Shen, A. Z. (2024). The synergy of elimination and zero-determinant strategy on dynamic games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that implementing endogenous reward and punishment policies in a regional public goods game increases both cooperation and total social welfare (W_t), especially when the intervention is targeted (partial intervention) rather than universal. The efficiency gains from punishment are largest when the cost of policy implementation is low or the punishment is strong. There is often an optimal fraction of groups to intervene in, beyond which additional intervention yields diminishing or negative returns due to policy costs. Size-based selection of groups for intervention is generally superior to random or degree-based selection. Allowing non-intervened groups to spontaneously adopt the policy can further boost cooperation and welfare, but only if the additional costs are offset by increased benefits. Terminating intervention leads to rapid collapse of cooperation and welfare, indicating the need for sustained policy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical/simulation evidence that enabling punishment (and reward) in a PGG-like environment increases efficiency (total social welfare), but the magnitude and even the sign of the effect depend on the cost of policy implementation, the strength of punishment, and the fraction of groups covered. For prediction, the model suggests that punishment will generally increase efficiency relative to control, especially when the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is not too low and policy costs are not too high. However, if policy costs are high or the environment is resource-poor (low mpcr), the efficiency gain may be small or even negative. The method of targeting intervention (e.g., size-based) can further moderate the effect. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG variants with spatial or regional structure and endogenous institutional punishment.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2024). Impact of dynamic compensation with resource feedback on the common pool resource game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that (1) static punishment (compensation) above a critical threshold leads to full cooperation, resource sustainability, and maximum group payoff; (2) dynamic punishment with resource feedback can promote cooperation and efficiency even when static punishment is insufficient, especially at low initial compensation intensity; (3) the relationship between punishment parameters and efficiency is non-monotonic and context-dependent, with oscillatory or convergent dynamics depending on parameter regions; (4) the proportion of fines used for ecological compensation (vs. other uses) should be tuned to maximize efficiency, with all fines used for compensation when feedback is high or initial intensity is high, and none when both are low.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that increasing punishment intensity (compensation) above a critical threshold sharply increases efficiency (group payoff) in common pool resource games. Dynamic, feedback-based punishment can further improve efficiency, especially when static punishment is weak. The results suggest that, for prediction, the presence and strength of punishment, as well as whether it is dynamically adjusted based on resource feedback, are key moderators of efficiency gains. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially for settings with resource feedback, variable punishment intensity, and allocation of fines to resource restoration.

- source: Wang, M., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2021). The role of alliance cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic common-pool resource game with probabilistic punishment of defectors, increasing the monitoring probability and punishment severity generally increases the proportion of cooperators and can help maintain higher resource levels (i.e., higher efficiency/welfare). However, if the initial resource level is too low or the resource's maximum growth rate is insufficient, the system will experience resource depletion (tragedy of the commons) regardless of punishment strength. The model demonstrates bistability: depending on initial conditions, the system may converge to full cooperation with resource depletion, or to a mixed/cooperative state with sustainable resources. Thus, punishment can improve efficiency, but only when ecological conditions allow for resource regeneration.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like resource environments is conditional: punishment (higher probability and severity) can increase efficiency and resource sustainability, but only if the resource's regenerative capacity and initial abundance are sufficient. In settings where the resource cannot recover (low growth rate or low initial stock), punishment does not prevent collapse. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by ecological parameters (resource growth, initial abundance) in addition to standard game design dimensions. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, and the efficiency outcome is directly modeled.

- source: Shutters, S. T. (2012). Punishment Leads to Cooperative Behavior in Structured Societies. *Evolutionary Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in agent-based simulations of a continuous prisoner's dilemma, cooperation (measured as mean contribution) does not evolve in the absence of both punishment and social structure. When only one of these is present, cooperation also does not evolve. However, when both social structure (local interactions on networks) and the option for costly punishment are present, populations can evolve to high levels of cooperation, depending on the strength of punishment and the network structure. The transition from defection to cooperation requires stronger punishment as network density increases. Scale-free networks are an exception, showing little or no response to punishment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, so direct implications for efficiency are not available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of both local social structure and effective punishment mechanisms is necessary for the evolution of high cooperation rates in continuous-contribution social dilemmas. However, because the outcome is mean contribution rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings must be interpreted as indirect evidence for efficiency effects. The results suggest that, in structured populations (except scale-free networks), enabling punishment can dramatically increase cooperation rates, which would likely increase efficiency, but the paper does not quantify this directly. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that punishment is only effective in structured (non-well-mixed) populations, and that the effectiveness of punishment depends on network density and punishment strength.

- source: Wang, Z., Li, R. D., Jin, X., & Ding, H. (2023). Emergence of Social Norms in Metanorms Game With High-Order Interaction Topology. *Ieee Transactions on Computational Social Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in metanorm games on uniform random hypergraphs (URH), increasing group size (G) promotes the emergence of social norms, leading to higher cooperation rates and more agents willing to punish defectors. Network connectivity (number of groups) does not affect norm emergence. On heterogeneous hypergraphs (HRH), standard BV-learning fails to establish norms for 'leaver' agents, but a new dynamic relevance BV-learning algorithm enables both hub and leaver agents to establish norms and cooperate. Real-world hypergraph data (coauthorship networks) show similar patterns. The main findings are about the conditions for norm emergence and high cooperation, not about efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting the effect of punishment on cooperation and norm compliance in public-goods-like games with high-order (group) interactions, especially regarding the role of group size and network structure. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that larger group size and effective learning algorithms for punishment/metapunishment can promote cooperation and norm compliance, which may be associated with higher efficiency in related settings, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Zhang, W., Zhao, D. K., Jin, X., Zhang, H. Z., An, T. B., Cui, G. H., & Wang, Z. (2025). Q-learning facilitates norm emergence in metanorm game model with topological structures. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a metanorm game with punishment and metapunishment, agents using self-regarding Q-learning can achieve high and robust cooperation rates (over 90% in many cases), even when agents lack information about others' payoffs. Punishment and metapunishment are essential for maintaining cooperation and suppressing defection and non-punishment. The model is robust to changes in network topology (scale-free, random, small-world), temptation payoff, population size, and observability. Highly connected agents are more likely to adopt punitive strategies. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this work.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment and metapunishment mechanisms can sustain high cooperation rates in social dilemma environments with local interactions and limited information, especially when agents use reinforcement learning. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The results are most useful for understanding the behavioral and dynamic effects of punishment and metapunishment, and for anticipating that high cooperation is possible under a wide range of design parameters, but not for quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: He, N. R., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2019). Central governance based on monitoring and reporting solves the collective-risk social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of central governance based on monitoring and reporting, with top-down punishment of defectors, can dramatically increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial collective-risk social dilemma. There are threshold effects: small increases in monitoring probability, punishment fine, or decreases in reporting cost can cause a sudden jump from low to high cooperation. The effect is stronger under harsh conditions (low synergy factor). However, the study focuses on cooperation rates, not on efficiency or group payoff, so direct implications for efficiency are not reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that top-down punishment based on monitoring and reporting can increase cooperation rates in spatial collective-risk dilemmas. However, because the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The findings suggest that, in similar spatial PGG-like environments, enabling punishment via monitoring/reporting is likely to increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency if contributions are productive, but the paper does not quantify this. The model's details (e.g., spatial structure, overlapping groups, no explicit cost of punishment to individuals) should be considered when generalizing.

- source: Cheng, H. H., Meng, X. Z., Hayat, T., & Hobiny, A. (2023). Multistability and bifurcation analysis for a three-strategy game system with public goods feedback and discrete delays. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reward can promote the survival of cooperators and the abundance of public goods, but only when their intensities are sufficiently high. Moderate reward and severe punishment are most conducive to cooperation. Excessively high reward does not always further increase cooperation. The cost of reward has a stronger negative effect on cooperation than the cost of punishment. Initial conditions and time delays can lead to multistability, oscillations, or extinction of cooperation. Large delays are detrimental to cooperation and can lead to the extinction of cooperators. The model highlights the complex interplay between feedback, incentives, and dynamic stability in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and numerical evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation and public good abundance is positive when punishment is sufficiently strong and not too costly, especially when combined with moderate reward. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its findings should be interpreted as supporting the idea that punishment can increase cooperation and public good provision under certain parameter regimes, but not as direct evidence for efficiency gains. The results suggest that the effect of punishment is sensitive to the costs of incentives, the presence of reward, and dynamic factors such as delays and initial conditions. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that punishment can improve group outcomes in public-goods-like settings, but the mapping to efficiency is indirect.

- source: Chica, M., & Santos, F. C. (2023). Seeding leading cooperators and institutions in networked climate dilemmas. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in networked collective-risk dilemmas with local punishment institutions, seeding a small fraction of punishers (institution supporters) at random or in network hubs can shift the system from low to high group achievement and institution prevalence, even when initial conditions are otherwise unchanged. Seeding cooperators is much less effective. The presence of punishment institutions is necessary for high cooperation; network structure alone does not suffice. Seeding network hubs is more expensive but not more effective than random seeding. The main outcomes are group achievement and institution prevalence, not direct efficiency or payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing punishment institutions (via seeding punishers) in a networked public-goods-like environment can strongly increase group-level cooperation outcomes (group achievement and institution prevalence), even when the control (no punishment) condition yields low cooperation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, its findings should be used as informative but indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. The results suggest that, in similar networked games, enabling punishment (especially via institutional mechanisms) is likely to increase efficiency if group achievement is a good proxy for efficiency. The paper also highlights that the effectiveness of punishment depends on both network structure and the presence of institutional mechanisms, and that seeding punishers is more effective than seeding cooperators.

- source: Yan, Z. Y., Zhao, H., Liang, S., Li, L., & Song, Y. J. (2024). Inter-layer feedback mechanism with reinforcement learning boosts the evolution of cooperation in multilayer network. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that adaptive punishment mechanisms, when combined with reinforcement learning in hypergraph-structured PGGs, substantially increase the fraction of cooperators. Key findings include: (1) higher punishment probability and intensity promote cooperation, (2) moderate punishment costs are optimal for sustaining cooperation, (3) smaller group sizes (hyperedges) facilitate earlier and higher cooperation, and (4) higher learning rates and discount factors in Q-learning further enhance cooperation. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model is theoretical and simulation-based, not empirical.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that adaptive punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in PGG-like environments, especially when punishment is likely, strong, and not too costly, and when group sizes are small. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are most useful for predicting how design dimensions (punishment cost, intensity, group size, etc.) affect cooperation rates, which may correlate with efficiency but are not equivalent. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides indirect, mechanistic support for the positive effect of punishment on cooperation, but not direct evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Griffin, C., & Belmonte, A. (2017). Cyclic public goods games: Compensated coexistence among mutual cheaters stabilized by optimized penalty taxation. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a cyclic public goods game with three species, the system without penalty taxation is unstable and leads to the dominance of a single species, with the interior coexistence point being a point of minimal total population growth (i.e., lowest efficiency). Imposing a penalty tax (punishment) on population growth can stabilize the interior fixed point and allow for coexistence of all species, but this tax is not redistributed and thus reduces overall efficiency further. The optimal tax is the minimal one that achieves stability, but any positive tax reduces total population growth below the already minimal level at the coexistence point. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include empirical or simulation data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in a cyclic public goods game with three types of mutual cheaters, introducing a penalty tax (punishment) can stabilize coexistence but always reduces total population growth (efficiency) compared to the no-punishment case. The penalty is not redistributed, so it is a pure cost. Thus, in similar game designs, enabling punishment (as a non-redistributive tax) is predicted to decrease efficiency relative to the control. The findings are most relevant for settings with cyclic/multispecies structure and non-redistributive punishment, and less so for standard two-strategy PGGs or for punishment that is redistributed. The results are directly informative for predicting the direction of efficiency change (less positive) when enabling punishment in such models.

- source: Wang, L. C., Liu, Y. Y., Guo, R. Q., Zhang, L., Liu, L. J., & Hua, S. J. (2024). Cooperation and resource sustainability in coupling social-ecological systems with dynamic growth rates. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that a peer rating-based heterogeneous distribution system (PRHDS) can promote cooperation in a structured population, but is fragile to random exploration (strategy noise). The main threat is the invasion of egoistic cooperators, which undermines prosocial clusters. Introducing monetary punishments for minority raters (either as a fixed fine or as a reduction in distribution share) can restore and stabilize cooperation, making the system robust to low levels of noise. Non-monetary punishment (reducing the voice of minority raters) is ineffective. The effect of the system depends on the matching of the public goods multiplier and the weight of ratings in the distribution formula. The model is all-or-nothing in both contribution and rating, and the main reported outcomes are behavioral (cooperation rates, strategy fractions), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It models a close variant of the public goods game with a peer rating-based distribution system, which acts as a combined reward/punishment mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible. However, the results suggest that monetary punishment mechanisms (with explicit costs or share reductions) can stabilize cooperation in noisy environments, while non-monetary punishments are ineffective. The model is all-or-nothing, with no chat, and the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is a key parameter. The findings suggest that, in similar designs, enabling monetary punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates (and thus, by implication, efficiency), especially when the system is subject to noise. The absence of direct efficiency outcomes limits the strength of inference for the prediction task.

- source: Zhou, K., & Ren, T. Y. (2021). Low-carbon technology collaborative innovation in industrial cluster with social exclusion: An evolutionary game theory perspective. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a social exclusion mechanism (where excluders pay a cost to expel defectors from the public goods game) can significantly increase the level of cooperation in a networked public goods environment. The effect is stronger when the cost of exclusion is low and the probability of successful exclusion is high. Sparse network structures (low average degree) are more conducive to sustaining cooperation. Voluntary participation (allowing loners) also helps prevent defection from dominating. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, focusing instead on the prevalence of cooperative strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (here, social exclusion) can increase cooperation rates in public-goods-like environments, especially when the cost of exclusion is low and the network is sparse. However, because the model and results focus on behavioral outcomes (cooperation level) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The model's structure and parameter explorations may inform qualitative expectations about when punishment is more likely to be effective, but do not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes for the prediction task.

- source: Song, Q., Cao, Z. H., Tao, R., Jiang, W., Liu, C., & Liu, J. Z. (2020). Conditional neutral punishment promotes cooperation in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that language and symbolic communication fundamentally alter the possibilities for collective action by simplifying coordination, reducing the costs of monitoring and punishing free riders, and amplifying reputation and signaling effects. It reviews theoretical models and empirical studies (from both ethnography and experiments) showing that communication (including chat, gossip, and feedback) increases cooperation rates and the effectiveness of punishment in social dilemmas. However, it does not report direct measures of efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with and without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that enabling communication (chat, language, gossip) in public goods games and collective action settings increases cooperation and the effectiveness of punishment, which should, in principle, improve group outcomes and efficiency. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct empirical evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it is not directly usable for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of communication and norm establishment as moderators of punishment effectiveness.

- source: Wu, B., García, J., Hauert, C., & Traulsen, A. (2013). Extrapolating Weak Selection in Evolutionary Games. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary games with finite populations, the ranking of strategies by abundance (e.g., cooperation, defection, punishment) can change as the intensity of selection increases. This is shown for public goods games with punishment as well as for general multiplayer and multi-strategy games. The main implication is that predictions based on weak selection (where payoffs have little effect on fitness) may not hold for moderate or strong selection. The paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, but rather focuses on which strategies are most common under different evolutionary conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. Instead, it shows that the abundance of strategies (including punishers) can change non-monotonically with selection intensity, and that results from weak selection may not extrapolate to stronger selection. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests caution in using weak selection results to predict efficiency effects of punishment, especially in games with more than two strategies or players. However, since efficiency or payoff outcomes are not analyzed, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency is limited.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Liu, Y., Zhao, J. H., & Gu, C. L. (2023). Evolutionary games with environmental feedbacks under an external incentive mechanism. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that external incentives (subsidies/carbon credits) can promote cooperation and improve environmental quality in a spatial public goods game with environmental feedback, but the effect is not always positive. Higher subsidies increase the competitiveness of cooperators (both those who reinvest in the public good and those who use credits for personal gain), but can also create conditions where defectors survive by exploiting improved environments, leading to coexistence or even dominance of defectors. The use of incentives to promote cooperation is thus sensitive to the balance between environmental repair and degradation rates, the structure of incentives, and the spatial dynamics of strategy clusters. The model does not include punishment or sanctions in the standard sense, but the 'second-order social dilemma' (where some cooperators act as free-riders on others' altruism) is analogous to the problem of sustaining costly punishment. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and environmental quality, with payoffs determined by these variables.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of external incentives (akin to rewards) on cooperation and group welfare in spatial public goods games with environmental feedback. It suggests that the effect of such mechanisms on efficiency or payoff is highly context-dependent: increasing the incentive can improve cooperation and welfare in some parameter regions, but can also reduce them in others due to complex spatial and strategic dynamics. The findings caution against assuming monotonic positive effects of incentives and highlight the importance of considering environmental feedbacks and the possibility of second-order free-riding. However, the model does not include punishment or sanctions as defined in the prediction task, so its direct applicability to punishment effects is limited. The results are most relevant for reward-based or subsidy-based interventions in spatial, environmentally-coupled PGGs.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2016). How the expanded crowd-funding mechanism of some southern rural areas in China affects cooperative behaviors in threshold public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that both publishing the list of cooperators and the presence of lobbyists can significantly increase the number of cooperators in a threshold public goods game, especially when the synergy factor is not too high. The effectiveness of these mechanisms depends on the population structure (proportion of unconditional cooperators vs. defectors), the synergy factor, and emotional influence parameters. When the synergy factor is high, publishing the list is always beneficial and the role of lobbyists becomes negligible. When the synergy factor is low, the decision to publish the list should consider the relative numbers of cooperators and defectors and the strength of emotional inhibition. The paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how social recognition (publishing the list) and persuasive agents (lobbyists) can increase cooperation rates in threshold public goods games, especially under certain parameter regimes. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment or similar mechanisms on efficiency. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral pathways by which social mechanisms affect cooperation, but should be used with caution for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Chen, W., Zhu, Q. L., & Wu, T. (2023). Unfairness promotes the evolution of cooperation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to reject unfair resource allocations (interpreted as a form of punishment) leads to higher levels of cooperation, especially when moderate unfairness is present. Both extreme fairness and extreme unfairness are suboptimal for cooperation. The mechanism is explained by cyclic dominance among strategy types. In continuous strategy settings, cooperators tend to act fairer than defectors, and clusters of cooperators can resist exploitation. The model is theoretical and simulation-based, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but rather focuses on the fraction of cooperators and the evolution of fairness/unfairness strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the ability to reject unfair offers (a form of costly punishment) can promote cooperation in public-goods-like games, especially when resource allocation is neither perfectly fair nor extremely unfair. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation rates and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is also theoretical and does not manipulate or report most of the game design dimensions used in the prediction task. Thus, while the paper supports the general idea that punishment for unfairness can promote cooperation, it does not provide direct quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency outcomes in empirical PGG experiments.

- source: Santos, F. C., Vasconcelos, V. V., Santos, M. D., Neves, P. N. B., & Pacheco, J. M. (2012). EVOLUTIONARY DYNAMICS OF CLIMATE CHANGE UNDER COLLECTIVE-RISK DILEMMAS. *Mathematical Models & Methods in Applied Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in collective-risk dilemmas (a variant of public goods games with a threshold and risk of collective loss), cooperation and group efficiency are maximized in small groups with high risk and stringent requirements for success. The evolutionary dynamics show that risk is a key driver for cooperation: without risk, defection dominates, but with increasing risk, stable coexistence or dominance of cooperation can emerge. The model provides analytical and simulation results for both infinite and finite populations, showing how group size, risk, and threshold interact to determine the likelihood of cooperation and the average payoff. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are modeled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting efficiency in threshold public goods games with collective risk, but it does not address the effect of punishment. It provides strong theoretical evidence that group size, risk level, and threshold requirements are key moderators of efficiency and cooperation. However, since punishment is not modeled, the paper does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to enabling punishment. Its results are most relevant for understanding baseline efficiency in collective-risk settings and for interpreting the effects of group size, risk, and threshold, but not for the incremental effect of punishment.

- source: Zhang, J. L., & Zhang, C. Y. (2015). The evolution of altruism in spatial threshold public goods games via an insurance mechanism. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in threshold public goods games, offering insurance to cooperators (but not defectors) can promote the prevalence of cooperative strategies, especially when the compensation for failed games is high and the threshold for success is high. However, if defectors are also allowed to purchase insurance, insured defection can become dominant, leading to a decrease in cooperative behaviors. The network structure (random regular vs. scale-free) also moderates these effects. The main outcomes are the steady-state fractions of each strategy; efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, but can be inferred from the modeled payoffs. The results suggest that insurance can substitute for some risk-mitigating effects of punishment, but the mechanism is distinct.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it studies insurance as a risk-mitigation mechanism rather than punishment or sanctions. However, it does provide indirect evidence about how risk-mitigation mechanisms (like insurance) can affect cooperation and, by implication, group welfare. The findings suggest that mechanisms which reduce the risk of loss for contributors (such as insurance) can promote cooperation, but if such mechanisms are also available to defectors, they may undermine cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper is most relevant for understanding the effects of risk and insurance, not punishment. It does not provide direct parameterizations for the target game design dimensions related to punishment.

- source: Hu, L. W., He, N. R., Weng, Q. F., Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2020). Rewarding endowments lead to a win-win in the evolution of public cooperation and the accumulation of common resources. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: Rewarding endowments in a collective-risk social dilemma (a PGG variant with environmental feedback) can create a win-win for cooperation and resource accumulation, but only in a specific parameter region: low basic endowment and intermediate reward intensity. High reward intensity or high basic endowment can lead to resource exhaustion, even if cooperation is high. The model does not include punishment or sanctions. The main outcomes are the amount of common resources (group welfare) and a composite index of cooperation and resource accumulation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, as it studies only reward mechanisms and does not include punishment or sanctions. However, it provides evidence that reward mechanisms can increase both cooperation and group welfare, but only under certain parameter regimes. The findings suggest that the design of incentive mechanisms (reward intensity, basic endowment) can have non-monotonic effects on group efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding the effects of reward as a moderator or alternative.

- source: Joshi, J., Brännström, & Dieckmann, U. (2020). Emergence of social inequality in the spatial harvesting of renewable public goods. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that when harvesting efficiency is low, all agents evolve to be sedentary and harvest at low rates, resulting in equitable but inefficient resource use. As harvesting efficiency increases, agents become mobile, and resource extraction approaches the maximum sustainable yield, achieving both efficiency and equity. Further increases in harvesting efficiency or reductions in dispersal cost lead to overexploitation and the emergence of social inequality, with a minority of mobile, overexploitative agents and a majority of frugal, sedentary agents. Rapid social learning (impatience) and local information (myopia) further aggravate inequality and inefficiency. The model identifies key moderators of efficiency and equity but does not include or analyze punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how efficiency in spatial public goods–like environments depends on ecological and behavioral parameters such as harvesting efficiency, dispersal cost, consumer density, and information structure. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctioning, as these mechanisms are absent from the model. Therefore, while the findings are useful for predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions and for understanding the role of mobility and social learning, the paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to the introduction of punishment. It is best used for baseline efficiency estimation and for understanding moderators of overexploitation and inequality in the absence of sanctions.

- source: Quan, J., Zhang, X. M., Chen, W. M., & Wang, X. J. (2023). Cooperation dynamics in collective risk games with endogenous endowments. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a two-stage PGG-CRG model with endogenous endowments, higher perceived risk and lower required contribution ratios increase the proportion of the population that successfully avoids catastrophic loss (target achieved rate). The effect of the first-stage enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR) is non-monotonic: when the required contribution ratio is small, higher enhancement factors help, but at moderate contribution ratios, a moderate enhancement factor is optimal. The model does not include or analyze punishment or sanction mechanisms, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or related outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or collective risk games, as no punishment or sanction mechanism is present in the model. However, it does provide informative context on how other game design parameters (risk, contribution ratio, enhancement factor) affect cooperation and group success in a threshold public goods/collective risk environment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment, but may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency or cooperation rates in the absence of punishment.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Zhao, J. H., Gu, C. L., & Chen, W. M. (2024). Evolutionary dynamics in spatial public goods games with environmental feedbacks. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study models a spatial public goods game with environmental feedback, where individuals can be cooperators (who pay a cost to improve local environmental quality) or defectors (who do not pay and degrade the environment). The model explores how the ability of cooperators to repair the environment (delta), the destructiveness of defectors (gamma), and the cost of cooperation (c) affect the prevalence of cooperation. Key findings include: (1) Cooperation can only dominate if cooperator repair ability slightly exceeds defector destructiveness; (2) Increasing cooperator repair ability beyond a threshold can paradoxically reduce cooperation due to defector invasions; (3) Both very low and very high defector destructiveness can undermine cooperation; (4) Increasing cooperation cost generally reduces cooperation, but at low defector destructiveness, higher cost can sometimes help cooperation by reducing isolated cooperators and making clusters more robust. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide detailed theoretical insights into how environmental feedback, spatial structure, and cost parameters affect the evolution of cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide contextual understanding of how non-punishment mechanisms and environmental feedbacks shape cooperation dynamics in spatial PGG variants.

- source: Wang, X. J., Ding, R., Zhao, J. H., Chen, W. M., & Gu, C. L. (2022). Competition of punishment and reward among inequity-averse individuals in spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that reputation-based indirect reciprocity can promote the spread of donation and cooperation behaviors in a donation game variant of the PGG, but this effect is limited by the costs of donation and cooperation, and by the presence of 'hypocrite' and 'realist' strategies. When only extreme altruists and egoists are present, cooperation rarely persists. The introduction of shunners (who conditionally cooperate based on reputation) allows for the spread of donation and cooperation under certain cost regimes. However, the addition of hypocrites (who donate for reputation but defect in daily interactions) and realists (who do not donate but cooperate in daily interactions) undermines the prevalence of donation, leading to more complex phase diagrams and sometimes the collapse of donation behavior. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of different strategies and the qualitative structure of phase transitions; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments, as it explicitly excludes punishment mechanisms and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about the role of indirect reciprocity (reputation) as an alternative to punishment in promoting cooperation, and about how the presence of certain strategy types and cost parameters can affect the prevalence of prosocial behaviors. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., reputation) can support cooperation under some conditions, but also that the presence of exploitative or non-donating cooperators can undermine these effects. The findings are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Zhou, Y. F., Zheng, X. M., & Wu, W. W. (2015). Effect of local information within network layers on the evolution of cooperation in duplex public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a D-PGG where agents allocate all their resources to one of two network layers, the use of local information for imitation (i.e., agents observe and imitate based on payoffs from a single layer) generally promotes the prevalence of cooperative strategies compared to global information. The extent of this promotion depends on the network structure and the similarity between the two network layers. The study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how information structure and network multiplexity affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff or efficiency outcomes, nor for the effect of punishment. It should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency, but may provide contextual background on how information and network structure can moderate cooperation.

- source: Hadzibeganovic, T., Lima, F. W. S., & Stauffer, D. (2012). Evolution of tag-mediated altruistic behavior in one-shot encounters on large-scale complex networks. *Computer Physics Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that tag-mediated cooperation can emerge and temporarily dominate in one-shot, multiplayer PD-like games on complex networks, especially when agents can recognize and preferentially help those with similar tags. The dominance of specific cooperative strategies (altruist vs. ethnocentric) depends on network topology (rewiring probability), but over long timescales, all strategies tend to equilibrate. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the prevalence of cooperation and strategy types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is relevant for understanding how tag-based recognition and network structure can support or suppress cooperation in the absence of punishment, but does not address payoff-based outcomes or the impact of sanctions. It may provide weak contextual information about baseline cooperation in one-shot, multiplayer PD-like environments without punishment, but is not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Wang, X. F., Chen, X. J., Gao, J., & Wang, L. (2013). Reputation-based mutual selection rule promotes cooperation in spatial threshold public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that a reputation-based mutual selection rule in spatial threshold public goods games can promote cooperation, with moderate tolerance ranges and lower member thresholds being most favorable for the persistence of cooperation. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and thus does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or related payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on cooperation rates under mutual selection and threshold group formation may be contextually informative for understanding how group composition and selection mechanisms affect cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency outcomes in games with or without punishment.

- source: Shi, Y. D., Zhong, L. X., & Xu, W. J. (2013). Effects of group sensitivity on cooperation in N-person snowdrift game with dynamic grouping. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in an N-person snowdrift game where agents can adjust their preferred group size based on group sensitivity (a parameter reflecting emotional response to group outcomes), moderate group sensitivity leads to the highest levels of cooperation. Both the cost-to-benefit ratio and group sensitivity affect the evolution of preferred group size and cooperation frequency. There is a functional relationship between the frequency of cooperators and the average preferred group size. However, the paper does not include punishment, sanctions, or direct efficiency/payoff analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It is relevant for understanding how group size dynamics and emotional factors (group sensitivity) can affect cooperation rates, but it does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Liu, Y. Y., Liu, L. J., Guo, R. Q., & Zhang, L. (2023). N-player repeated evolutionary trust game under government management. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a government-managed punishment and reward mechanism in a repeated N-player trust game can sustain trust and investment, leading to higher group payoffs. There is a critical threshold of punishment intensity: below it, untrustworthy behavior dominates and payoffs are low; above it, trust and investment are maintained, and payoffs increase. The model also shows that as punishment intensity increases further, the system can reach a stable coexistence of strategies with high trust and investment. The results are robust across different group sizes and parameter values, and are supported by both theoretical analysis and numerical simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated trust-game-like environments with group structure, introducing or increasing the intensity of punishment (and associated rewards for reporting) can substantially increase group efficiency/payoff, provided the punishment is above a critical threshold. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, so the qualitative prediction that punishment increases efficiency is likely to transfer to PGG-like settings, especially those with repeated interactions and third-party enforcement. The results are most informative for settings where punishment is implemented by an external authority and is contingent on reporting.

- source: Tao, J., Zhong, X., & Lu, Y. F. (2011). Restrictive mechanism of flow control among non-cooperative Internet users. *Science China-information Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated flow control games, the use of punishment strategies (including trigger, tit-for-tat, and finite punishment) can deter selfish deviations and sustain cooperation among users, leading to optimal or near-optimal system utility. In the absence of such mechanisms, the equilibrium is inefficient. The FCAR algorithm, which operationalizes finite punishment, is shown via simulation to effectively regularize user behavior and maintain high system utility, with only temporary utility losses during punishment. The effectiveness of punishment depends on the number of users and the length of punishment. In finitely repeated games, cooperation is harder to sustain, especially in the final stage, but rational punishment strategies can still maintain cooperation for most of the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment in repeated resource allocation games (adjacent to public goods games) increases efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust to the number of users, provided the punishment is sufficiently strong and the game is sufficiently long or indefinite. The findings support the prediction that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment will increase average efficiency, especially when users are patient (high discount factor) and punishment is coordinated. The results are most directly applicable to repeated, continuous-action, non-anonymous settings with full information about others' actions and payoffs.

- source: Quan, J., Pu, Z. J., & Wang, X. J. (2021). Comparison of social exclusion and punishment in promoting cooperation: Who should play the leading role?. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both keeping and reversing social norms can promote cooperation and increase social welfare (average payoff) in indirect reciprocity games with punishment. The effect is moderated by the benefit-cost ratio: keeping rules are more effective at low benefit-cost ratios, while reversing rules are more effective at high benefit-cost ratios. Public reputation scenarios consistently support higher cooperation and welfare than private reputation scenarios. Punishment actions can help sustain cooperation, but their cost can reduce average welfare. The specific social norm in use (among 16 studied) determines whether the population reaches a cooperative or non-cooperative stable state, especially in private reputation settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of social norms and the structure of reputation (public vs. private) are critical moderators of the effect of punishment on efficiency (average payoff) in indirect reciprocity games. The results suggest that, in environments similar to pairwise donation games with indirect reciprocity, enabling punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency, but the net effect on efficiency depends on the cost of punishment and the social norm in use. Public reputation and appropriate social norms (keeping or reversing, depending on benefit-cost ratio) are important for maximizing efficiency gains from punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to pairwise donation games, not standard public goods games, but the mechanisms are adjacent and informative for prediction.

- source: Thomadsen, R., & Bhardwaj, P. (2011). Cooperation in Games with Forgetfulness. *Management Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of forgetfulness on efficiency in repeated games with punishment is fundamentally ambiguous: it can either reduce or increase the set of discount factors and game parameters under which full cooperation (and thus maximum efficiency) is sustainable. When the stage game is such that the cooperative action always yields nonnegative payoffs, forgetfulness weakens punishment and makes cooperation harder to sustain, reducing efficiency. However, when the stage game allows for the possibility that a forgetful player can be punished below the minimax, forgetfulness can make severe punishments credible and thus expand the range of parameters supporting cooperation, increasing efficiency. The paper provides explicit formulas and graphical illustrations for these effects in the repeated prisoner's dilemma and a franchising effort game. The results are theoretical and depend on the structure of the stage game and the probability of forgetfulness.
