  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated games is not monotonic and can be moderated by cognitive or institutional features like forgetfulness. In environments where punishment is only as severe as the stage minimax, introducing forgetfulness will generally reduce the efficiency gains from punishment. However, in environments where forgetfulness allows for below-minimax punishments, punishment can become more effective at sustaining cooperation, potentially increasing efficiency beyond what is possible with perfect recall. For prediction tasks, this means that the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined solely from standard game design dimensions; one must also consider whether the environment allows for forgetfulness and whether the stage game payoffs permit below-minimax punishments. The paper does not provide empirical estimates but gives clear theoretical boundaries and mechanisms.

- source: Evans, R., & Thomas, J. P. (2001). Cooperation and punishment. *Econometrica*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated common interest games (with no discounting), the mere presence of cooperative (nondraconian) strategies in the set of possible strategies does not guarantee efficient (Pareto-optimal) outcomes in equilibrium. However, if the set of possible strategies includes at least one 'draconian' strategy (i.e., a strategy that is willing to punish noncooperation to the maximum extent, such as min-maxing the opponent forever after a deviation), then all Nash equilibria are efficient. The paper also shows that this result is robust to certain computability restrictions and discusses the limits of these results under discounting and other technical assumptions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in repeated games with a structure similar to public goods or common interest games, the availability of sufficiently severe punishment (i.e., the ability to impose maximal punishment at low or no cost) is both necessary and sufficient for punishment to induce efficient outcomes in equilibrium. If the game design allows for draconian punishment strategies, then enabling punishment should lead to high efficiency, regardless of the control efficiency. If only mild or 'cooperative' punishment is possible, efficiency gains are not guaranteed. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, infinite-horizon, undiscounted games, but the logic is relevant for understanding the role of punishment severity and credibility in other settings.

- source: Mondal, S. S., Pathak, M., & Chakraborty, S. (2022). Reward versus punishment: averting the tragedy of the commons in eco-evolutionary dynamics. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reward can prevent the tragedy of the commons in a dynamic resource game, but their effects on efficiency differ. Sufficiently strong punishment leads to a stable equilibrium with all cooperators and a fully replete resource, achieving the social optimum (maximum possible group payoff). In contrast, even with strong reward, some defectors persist, so the group does not reach the maximum efficiency. The model also shows that the effect of punishment or reward depends on the initial state and the intensity of the intervention, and that bistability (multiple possible long-run outcomes) can occur. The analysis is theoretical, using replicator dynamics and stability analysis, and is not based on empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in dynamic resource games with feedback (and by extension, in public-goods-like environments), enabling punishment (with sufficient intensity) can increase group efficiency and achieve the social optimum, while enabling reward alone cannot fully eliminate inefficiency due to persistent defectors. The results are most directly applicable to settings where the game is a prisoner's dilemma with resource feedback, and where punishment or reward is implemented as a direct modification of payoffs. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment should be expected to increase efficiency more than enabling reward, especially when the goal is to reach the maximum possible group payoff. However, the model is theoretical and does not address practical implementation details or empirical constraints.

- source: McBride, M., Kendall, R., D'Orsogna, M. R., & Short, M. B. (2016). Crime, punishment, and evolution in an adversarial game. *European Journal of Applied Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, unlike standard social dilemma games (such as the public goods game), the adversarial game studied here can have multiple equilibria, including both efficient (no-crime, high-payoff) and inefficient (high-crime, low-payoff) outcomes. The presence of punishment (reporting and conviction) transforms the game from a pure social dilemma into a coordination game, where the effectiveness of punishment depends on the collective willingness to report. The Informant strategy (defectors who also report) is critical: under imitation dynamics, any presence of Informants is sufficient to reach efficiency, but under best response dynamics, Informants are necessary but not sufficient. The model shows that too severe punishment or high retaliation risk can undermine the emergence of efficient equilibria. Thus, the design of punishment institutions and the social context (e.g., support for reporting) are crucial moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like adversarial games is highly sensitive to the details of the punishment mechanism, the available strategy set (especially the presence of Informants), and the evolutionary dynamics. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, but only under certain parameter regimes: moderate punishment severity, low retaliation risk, and the availability of strategies that combine defection and punishment. The findings caution against assuming that punishment will always improve efficiency; instead, the effect is conditional and can be negative or null if punishment is too harsh or if key strategies are unavailable. The results are most directly applicable to adversarial games with crime and reporting, but the mechanism insights may transfer to public goods games with similar punishment structures.

- source: Gámez, M., López, I., Rodríguez, C., Varga, Z., & Garay, J. (2018). Game-theoretical model for marketing cooperative in fisheries. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that effective punishment (penalty parameter β > 1) stabilizes full cooperation in a marketing cooperative, leading to the group achieving the maximum possible profit (i.e., efficiency). When punishment is limited (β = 1), cooperation is unstable and members tend to defect, reducing group profit. The model is dynamic, with payoffs and strategies evolving over time, but the key result is that sufficiently strong punishment ensures convergence to the efficient outcome. The findings are demonstrated both analytically and through simulation examples.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling sufficiently strong punishment in a public-goods-like environment (here, a marketing cooperative) will increase efficiency, moving the group toward the maximum possible collective payoff. The model is not a standard PGG but is structurally similar: members choose how much to contribute to the group (by selling through the cooperative), and the group can punish defectors. The explicit analysis of payoff functions and the demonstration that efficiency is achieved only when punishment is strong enough (β > 1) are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, especially in settings where the punishment technology is effective and the cost/impact ratio is favorable. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide empirical effect sizes.

- source: Liu, S. Y., Zhang, C. Y., Li, K., & Zhang, J. L. (2022). Exploring the inducement for social dilemma and cooperation promotion mechanisms in structured populations. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study shows that in structured populations playing iterated prisoner's dilemma with memory-one strategies, increased randomness and heterogeneity in network topology (e.g., moving from regular lattice to scale-free networks) lead to a higher prevalence of defectors and lower collective average payoff (social welfare). The key behavioral parameter is p4, the probability of cooperating after mutual defection; low p4 is associated with 'dangerous' strategies that dominate in more random/heterogeneous networks and drive payoffs toward the minimum. The authors introduce two mechanisms: (1) enforcement, which forbids p4 < 0.3, and (2) punishment, which imposes a fine on agents with p4 < 0.3. Both mechanisms, especially with sufficient punishment intensity, eliminate dangerous strategies and raise the collective average payoff substantially, regardless of network structure. The effect is robust across random regular, ER random, and BA scale-free networks.
  prediction_guidance: Although the paper studies the iterated prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game, the structure is closely related, and the main outcome—collective average payoff as a fraction of the maximum possible—is directly analogous to efficiency. The findings provide strong evidence that introducing punishment (with sufficient intensity) in a repeated social dilemma robustly increases group efficiency, especially in environments where network structure would otherwise favor defection. The results are most applicable to settings where punishment targets low willingness to cooperate after mutual defection. The paper also highlights that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its strength: weak punishment may not eliminate low-cooperation strategies, but moderate to strong punishment does. These insights should inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment in repeated, networked social dilemmas, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low due to network-induced defection.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2017). Characteristics of the evolution of cooperation by the probabilistic peer-punishment based on the difference of payoff. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a probabilistic peer-punishment mechanism—where the probability and severity of punishment are proportional to payoff differences—can increase both cooperation and the average payoff of all players in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is robust across different network topologies (regular, random, scale-free) and for varying average degrees of connectivity, though the parameter range for positive effects narrows as connectivity increases. The model avoids antisocial punishment by design. The results suggest that, with appropriate parameterization, punishment can enhance group efficiency, not just cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in environments structurally similar to spatial prisoner's dilemma games, a well-designed probabilistic peer-punishment mechanism can increase group efficiency (average payoff), especially when the punishment is neither too weak nor too strong. The effect is sensitive to the network structure: as the average number of connections increases, the range of punishment parameters that yield efficiency gains becomes narrower. While the model is not a public goods game, the payoff-based outcome and the punishment mechanism are closely related, making the findings informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like settings, especially where network structure and punishment calibration are relevant.

- source: Luo, J. S., & Zhao, M. (2013). Proportional cost for punishment enhances spatial reciprocity in evolutionary games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that proportional punishment (where the cost is distributed across a proportion of the population and defectors are fined based on their payoff) enhances both cooperation and average payoff in spatial prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games. As the proportion of the population bearing punishment cost (p) and the strength of punishment (beta) increase, the average payoff rises and more individuals achieve higher payoffs. The effect is robust across both game types. The paper provides detailed analysis of how payoff distributions shift with punishment parameters, showing that punishment reduces the number of individuals with very low or very high payoffs, concentrating payoffs in a higher, narrower range.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatial pairwise games (PDG and SD), introducing a proportional punishment mechanism increases average payoff (efficiency) compared to the baseline without punishment. The effect is stronger as the proportion of the population involved in punishment and the punishment strength increase. While the setting is not a public goods game, the adjacent nature of the games and the explicit analysis of average payoff and its distribution make the findings relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar environments. The results suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment (especially with higher coverage and strength) will increase efficiency relative to the control. However, the model is theoretical and does not address all PGG design dimensions (e.g., no chat, no reward, no explicit round structure), so direct quantitative transfer should be cautious.

- source: Cai, G. S., & Kock, N. (2009). An evolutionary game theoretic perspective on e-collaboration: The collaboration effort and media relativeness. *European Journal of Operational Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player e-collaboration games, introducing or increasing social punishment for defection increases the likelihood of full cooperation, which is Pareto efficient and maximizes group payoff. When punishment is low, defection or asymmetric cooperation is stable; when punishment exceeds a threshold (typically half the cost of cooperation), full cooperation becomes the unique stable Nash equilibrium. This result holds in both discrete and continuous effort models. The paper also shows that the required punishment to induce cooperation decreases as collaboration efficiency increases. The analysis is entirely theoretical, with explicit payoff formulas and equilibrium characterizations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling or increasing punishment in a two-player, public-goods-like game will increase efficiency, provided the punishment is sufficiently strong relative to the cost of cooperation. The analytic results specify the threshold at which punishment changes the equilibrium from defection to cooperation. While the model is for two players and not a standard PGG, the structure is closely related, and the findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in small-group cooperation games. The results are most applicable when the punishment mechanism is salient, effective, and the cost/benefit structure is similar to the model.

- source: Wang, S. X., Cao, M., & Chen, X. J. (2025). Optimally Combined Incentive for Cooperation Among Interacting Agents in Population Games. *Ieee Transactions on Automatic Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the optimal protocol for combining reward and punishment to promote cooperation is time-invariant and depends on the cost-to-benefit ratio and the initial and target cooperation levels. When the initial cooperation level is high, pure punishment is most cost-effective; when it is low, pure reward is more efficient; and for intermediate cases, a specific mix of reward and punishment minimizes the total cost. The minimal cost protocol is analytically derived for both well-mixed (complete graph) and regular network populations. The results are robust across different network structures. The findings are directly relevant to predicting the efficiency of institutional punishment and/or reward interventions in repeated social dilemma settings, though the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma rather than the public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency of punishment (and reward) interventions in repeated social dilemma games, especially when the outcome of interest is the cost required to achieve a target level of cooperation. While the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma rather than the public goods game, the structure and logic of institutional incentives are closely related. The results suggest that the efficiency of punishment depends critically on the initial cooperation level, the cost-to-benefit ratio, and the network structure. The paper does not report group payoff or surplus in the standard PGG sense, but its focus on cumulative cost as a measure of efficiency is highly relevant for predicting the cost-effectiveness of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Zhang, Y., Hao, Q. Y., Qian, J. L., Wu, C. Y., & Bi, Y. (2024). The influence of the heterogeneities of social institutions and individuals' tendency to establish social institutions on cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneous social institutions (pool punishment, anti-social punishment, and social security/reward) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma framework can promote the evolution of pro-social groups and suppress second-order free-riding. The model shows that increasing the cost of institutional investment and the growth factor of the institutional pool generally promotes cooperation and higher average payoffs, but only up to an optimal point. The introduction of a non-wasteful model with an upper limit on individual punishment/reward further improves the efficiency of the system by reducing resource waste and allowing pro-social groups to survive under harsher social dilemmas. The network (spatial) structure enhances these effects compared to well-mixed populations. While the main outcomes are strategy proportions, the paper also analyzes average payoffs, showing that institutional punishment and reward mechanisms can increase group welfare compared to baseline scenarios without such institutions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of punishment and reward institutions—including their cost, effectiveness, and resource allocation rules—can significantly affect the efficiency (average payoff) of groups in social dilemma games. While the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma rather than a standard public goods game, the mechanisms are closely related, especially for pool punishment and institutional reward. The findings suggest that enabling punishment (and reward) institutions, especially with non-wasteful resource allocation and optimal parameter settings, will generally increase group efficiency compared to control conditions without such institutions. The results also highlight the importance of network structure, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the presence of anti-social punishment as moderators. However, the lack of direct efficiency ratios and the use of a prisoner's dilemma rather than a PGG limit the direct quantitative transferability to PGG-like environments.

- source: Ntemos, K., Pikramenos, G., & Kalouptsidis, N. (2022). Dynamic Information Sharing and Punishment Strategies. *Ieee Transactions on Automatic Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in dynamic information sharing games with two agents, cooperation (information sharing) is not sustainable in finite-horizon settings, but can be sustained in infinite-horizon settings using punishment strategies (CGT). The equilibrium region where cooperation is optimal is characterized, and its size increases as the cost of sharing decreases or the discount factor increases. Theoretical results show that the maximal equilibrium region is unique and can be computed iteratively. Simulations confirm that lower costs and more informative observations lead to larger cooperation regions. Thus, enabling punishment strategies increases the efficiency of the system (total expected information gain minus cost) compared to the baseline where no sharing occurs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment strategies in repeated/dynamic information sharing games can increase efficiency (total payoff) compared to the baseline with no punishment, provided the game is sufficiently long (infinite horizon) and the cost of sharing is not too high. The effect is more positive when the cost of sharing is low and the discount factor is high. The model is adjacent to public goods games: the 'public good' is information, and the payoff is information gain minus cost. The results suggest that, in similar dynamic games, enabling punishment will increase efficiency, especially as the game becomes more repeated and the cost of cooperation decreases. However, the model is limited to two players, binary actions, and does not include monetary payoffs or group sizes greater than two.

- source: Zhu, Y. Y., Zhang, J. L., Sun, Q. L., & Chen, Z. Q. (2017). Evolutionary dynamics of strategies for threshold snowdrift games on complex networks. *Knowledge-based Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment in threshold snowdrift games increases the evolutionary advantage of cooperation over defection, especially when punishment cost is low and the threshold is moderate. Larger thresholds generally promote cooperation, but too high or too low thresholds can be detrimental. Lower punishment cost (to the punisher) and higher punishment impact (on the defector) both facilitate cooperation. These effects are robust across well-mixed and networked populations, including single-layer and double-layer (interconnected) networks. However, the main reported outcomes are cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can increase cooperation rates in threshold public-goods-like games, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly. However, it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. Theoretical payoff difference (P_c - P_d) is analyzed, but simulation results focus on cooperation rates. The findings suggest that punishment is more effective when the cost to punishers is low and the penalty to defectors is high, and that network structure and threshold size moderate these effects. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment can improve cooperation under certain parameter regimes, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence on efficiency.

- source: Wang, J. F., & Guo, J. L. (2019). A synergy of punishment and extortion in cooperation dilemmas driven by the leader. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a networked donation game, a single high-degree leader node using a combined punishment and extortion strategy can induce full cooperation in the population with probability close to 1, regardless of initial conditions or the presence of other strategies. This combined strategy yields the highest group and leader payoffs, and is robust to variations in the temptation to defect and the set of available strategies. Pure punishment or pure extortion by the leader is less effective. The results suggest a strong synergy between punishment and extortion in promoting cooperation and maximizing group welfare in networked social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in networked prisoner's dilemma/donation games, the presence of a punishment mechanism—especially when combined with extortion and wielded by a central leader—can dramatically increase group payoff and the probability of full cooperation. While the setting is not a standard public goods game, the findings are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments with network structure and leader influence. The results suggest that enabling punishment (especially in combination with other mechanisms) can shift efficiency from low to near-maximal, even when baseline cooperation is low. However, the effect depends on the presence of influential nodes and may not generalize to well-mixed or non-networked settings.

- source: Shen, C., Song, Z., Shi, L., Tanimoto, J., & Wang, Z. (2025). Exit options sustain altruistic punishment and decrease the second-order free-riders, but it is not a panacea. *Science China-information Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that adding an exit option to a prisoner's dilemma with altruistic punishment changes the evolutionary dynamics. In well-mixed populations, exiters replace defectors as the exit payoff increases, but altruistic punishers do not become dominant. In networked populations, exiters can support the coexistence of altruistic punishers, defectors, and exiters via cyclic dominance, but this effect is unstable and depends on the exit payoff. Sometimes, non-punishing cooperators replace altruistic punishers, and the system can exhibit bi-stability between these outcomes. The effect of exiters on cooperation and punishment is nuanced and context-dependent. The paper does not provide direct predictions or measurements of group efficiency or welfare, but the results suggest that exit options can sometimes support, but also sometimes undermine, the mechanisms that sustain cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how the presence and incentive level of an exit option (voluntary participation) can affect the evolutionary stability of punishment and cooperation in social dilemmas. While the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, it suggests that exit options can destabilize defection and, under some conditions in networked populations, support the coexistence of altruistic punishment. However, the effect is highly sensitive to the exit payoff and population structure, and exiters can also undermine punishment by enabling non-punishing cooperators to dominate. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of an exit option and its incentive level are important moderators of punishment's effect, especially in structured populations, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Matsuzawa, R., & Tanimoto, J. (2018). Sanctions triggered by jealousy help promote the cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that jealousy-motivated sanctions (where agents punish neighbors with higher payoffs, regardless of strategy) can promote cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. The effect depends on the network structure: in lattice networks, higher sanction efficiency (penalty per cost) promotes cooperation, while in scale-free networks, lower efficiency (higher cost per penalty) can be more effective. However, the paper notes that even when cooperation is achieved, the continued use of jealous sanctions (due to persistent payoff inequalities) can reduce total social payoff, potentially lowering efficiency. The main reported outcome is the cooperation fraction, not direct group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, but the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game. The main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation, but may not always improve efficiency due to the cost of sanctions, especially when punishment is motivated by jealousy and can be directed at cooperators as well as defectors. The effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to depend on network structure and the cost-effectiveness of punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that punishment may increase cooperation but could reduce efficiency if sanctioning is costly and not well-targeted.

- source: Yang, Q. X., & Yang, Y. L. (2025). Effects of an update mechanism based on combinatorial memory and high-reputation learning objects on the evolution of. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of a social monitoring mechanism (SMM) that rewards judges based on the observed cooperation rate among participants leads to higher levels of cooperation and lower corruption in the simulated two-layer network. The effect is robust across different parameter settings, including reward/fine amounts, the social reward criterion, the presence of zealots, and the strength of the reward factor. SMM is more effective than simply increasing rewards/fines in the absence of SMM. The model is applied to both Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift Game variants, showing similar qualitative results. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation/corruption rates).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which reward third-party punishers based on group cooperation rates can increase cooperation and reduce corruption in environments structurally similar to the Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift Game. However, because the outcomes are reported as cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results should be interpreted as informative about behavioral mechanisms rather than direct efficiency effects. The model is not a standard public goods game, but the two-layer structure and the focus on punishment/reward mechanisms are adjacent to PGG-like environments. The findings suggest that adding a social monitoring/reward mechanism for punishers may increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency is not directly quantified.

- source: Pan, Q. H., Wang, Y., & He, M. F. (2022). Impacts of special cooperation strategy with reward and punishment mechanism on cooperation evolution. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing special cooperators who can both reward and punish leads to a variety of stable states, including coexistence of all three strategies or pairs of strategies. Punishment is necessary to suppress defectors and enable cooperation, but excessive punishment can reduce the prevalence of special cooperators. Rewards only promote ordinary cooperation when punishment is at least moderate. The effects of reward and punishment on cooperation are non-linear: moderate levels are most effective, while too much of either can be counterproductive. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so findings are limited to behavioral strategy frequencies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence that punishment (especially when combined with reward) can promote the prevalence of cooperative strategies in spatial evolutionary games. However, because the outcomes are proportions of strategy types rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task. The findings suggest that moderate punishment is most effective for promoting cooperation, and that the effect of reward depends on the level of punishment. For prediction of efficiency, this paper suggests that the relationship between punishment and efficiency may be non-monotonic, with moderate punishment being optimal, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Lee, H. W., Cleveland, C., & Szolnoki, A. (2024). Suppressing defection by increasing temptation: The impact of smart cooperators on a social dilemma situation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that adding a 'smart cooperator' strategy (who both punishes defectors and rewards cooperators, at a cost) to a spatial prisoner's dilemma model leads to cyclic dominance among defectors, cooperators, and smart cooperators. This cyclic dominance produces counter-intuitive effects: for example, increasing the temptation to defect can reduce the prevalence of defectors by indirectly supporting smart cooperators. Similarly, increasing punishment does not always reduce defection, and lowering the cost of smart cooperation can benefit unconditional cooperators more than smart cooperators. The main reported outcomes are the stationary fractions of each strategy, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how punishment and reward mechanisms interact in a spatial social dilemma with multiple strategies, but does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that the introduction of complex sanctioning strategies can lead to non-linear and sometimes counter-intuitive effects on cooperation and defection rates, but the lack of efficiency or payoff reporting limits its direct utility for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments. The main value is in highlighting the potential for cyclic dominance and unexpected behavioral dynamics when multiple sanctioning strategies are present.

- source: Zhao, S. S., Pan, Q. H., Zhu, W. Q., & He, M. F. (2023). How punishing evil and promoting good promotes cooperation in social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of a 'punishing evil and promoting good' strategy can promote the survival of cooperation in social dilemmas. Increasing punishment strength alone can induce the emergence of this strategy and ordinary cooperators, but is not always sufficient to eliminate defectors. Increasing reward strength can shift the system from full defection to full cooperation when the temptation to defect is low, but is insufficient when temptation is high. The most robust promotion of cooperation occurs when both punishment and reward are present, with certain combinations of minimum values able to eliminate defectors entirely. The results are presented as equilibrium proportions of each strategy, not as efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that both punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in social dilemma settings, and that their effects are complementary. However, because the outcomes are reported as equilibrium strategy frequencies rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is adjacent to public goods games (it is a prisoner's dilemma variant), and the findings suggest that the presence and strength of punishment and reward mechanisms are important moderators of cooperation, but do not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency.

- source: Wu, Y. E., Li, Z. Q., Liu, Z. Y., Cui, B. W., & Yin, A. R. (2024). Third party interventions promote cooperation on the interdependent networks: A perspective based on prospect theory. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of third-party intervention (punishment and/or reward) in a networked PD/SD game increases the fraction of cooperators in both the intervention and dispute layers. The effect is stronger when the intervention cost is lower and the intervention intensity (punishment/reward coefficients) is higher. The results are robust across different network topologies (square lattice and ER random networks) and game types (PD and SD). However, the paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare; all results are in terms of cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that third-party punishment and reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in networked social dilemmas, and that the effect is moderated by the cost and intensity of intervention. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The results are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not payoffs. The model is also based on the PD/SD rather than a standard PGG, and is simulation/theory-based rather than empirical.

- source: Huang, Y. K., & Inohara, T. (2015). Group-separations based on the repeated prisoners' dilemma games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper provides a theoretical framework for understanding how the ability to sustain cooperation via punishment (trigger strategies) in repeated pairwise prisoners' dilemma games depends on the discount factor and the players' mixed strategies. It shows that as the discount factor increases, more pairs can sustain cooperation, leading to larger groups of mutually cooperating players. The main findings are the mathematical characterization of the group-separation structure as a function of the discount factor, and the identification of intervals where complete-group-separations (where all within-group pairs can sustain cooperation) are possible. However, the paper does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants, as it does not analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its main contribution is theoretical: it shows how the ability to sustain cooperation via punishment in repeated pairwise games depends on the discount factor and the heterogeneity of players' strategies. For prediction tasks, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) is sensitive to the discount factor and the similarity of players' strategies, but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on efficiency effects. The model is also for pairwise repeated PDs, not for n-player public goods games.

- source: Yan, Z. Y., Li, L., Shang, J., & Zhao, H. (2024). Periodic update rule with Q-learning promotes evolution of cooperation in transition with mechanism. *Neurocomputing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing a punishment mechanism, where cooperators can choose to become punishers at a cost, increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is strongest when the punishment cost is low and the update period for strategy revision is short. The Q-learning-based periodic update rule (switching between Fermi and aspiration-driven rules) further enhances cooperation. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model is not a public goods game but a spatial pairwise prisoner's dilemma with punishment and learning dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial social dilemmas, especially when punishment is not too costly and agents update strategies frequently. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it should be used only as weak, adjacent evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The findings suggest that low punishment cost and frequent update opportunities are likely to make punishment more effective at promoting cooperation, but the translation to efficiency is uncertain.

- source: Xie, Y. Y., Zhang, S. H., Zhang, Z. P., & Bu, H. Y. (2020). Impact of binary social status with hierarchical punishment on the evolution of cooperation in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing only a subset of agents (the 'powerful' group) to punish defectors can promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma, especially when the punishment fine is sufficiently high. However, the relationship is non-linear: too many powerful agents or excessively strong punishment can reduce cooperation. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of hierarchical punishment on cooperation rates in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent to public goods games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment. The results suggest that the structure and exclusivity of punishment rights, as well as the intensity of punishment, can have non-monotonic effects on cooperation, which may be relevant for interpreting similar mechanisms in public goods games. Use this paper for qualitative or mechanistic insight, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Wu, Y., Zhang, B., & Zhang, S. H. (2017). Probabilistic reward or punishment promotes cooperation in evolutionary games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The introduction of a third-party agent who probabilistically rewards cooperators and punishes defectors increases the fraction of cooperators in both the prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games across different network structures. The effect is stronger as the reward/punishment magnitude increases and as the probability of encountering the third-party agent increases. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The results are robust across square lattice, Erdös-Rényi, and scale-free networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that probabilistic punishment (and/or reward) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma games, but it does not report on efficiency or group payoff. The model is not a public goods game, but a two-player prisoner's dilemma or snowdrift game, so direct prediction of efficiency in PGGs is not possible. The findings may be useful for understanding the general effect of third-party sanctions on cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Yang, H. X., & Chen, X. J. (2018). Promoting cooperation by punishing minority. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a multilayer network with inter-layer feedback and reinforcement learning, cooperation is promoted by lower punishment costs and higher punishment severity. The frequency of cooperation is sensitive to these parameters, with cooperation collapsing when punishment is too costly or too weak. The self-adapting Q-learning mechanism enables agents to learn strategies that support higher cooperation rates, especially when learning rates and discount factors are tuned appropriately. However, the main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment cost and severity affect cooperation rates in a spatially structured, multilayer environment with reinforcement learning. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation under certain cost/severity regimes, but excessive punishment cost can undermine cooperation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper's results are only indirectly informative, as it does not quantify group payoff or efficiency. The model is also not a standard public goods game, but rather a spatial prisoner's dilemma with punishment administered by a management layer.

- source: Gao, L., Li, Y. T., Wang, Z., & Wang, R. W. (2022). Asymmetric strategy setup solve the Prisoner's Dilemma of the evolution of mutualism. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing asymmetric carrot-stick strategies (where one population can reward or punish the other) leads to a rich set of evolutionary outcomes, including stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, bistability, and dominance, depending on parameter values. The presence and strength of punishment and reward (relative to benefit) are key moderators. The model suggests that asymmetric sanctions can help maintain cooperation and avoid the tragedy of the commons, especially when the cost of cooperation is high. However, the analysis is entirely theoretical and focuses on strategy frequencies and stability, not on group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the availability and strength of punishment and reward mechanisms (especially when asymmetric) can qualitatively change the evolutionary dynamics of cooperation. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that punishment (and reward) can stabilize cooperation under certain parameter regimes. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that the presence and magnitude of punishment and reward (relative to benefit and cost) are important moderators, but it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding the conditions under which punishment/reward can sustain cooperation, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Guo, T., Du, C. P., & Shi, L. (2024). Evolution of cooperation on interdependent networks: The impact of asymmetric punishment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that asymmetric punishment, where only one group can punish another and only across network layers, can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators in spatial prisoner's dilemma games on interdependent networks. The effect is stronger when the punishment is more severe (higher fine per unit cost). When the temptation to defect is low, punishment is more effective than reward at promoting cooperation; when the temptation is high, reward becomes more effective. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The paper does not report efficiency or total group payoff, so its findings are most directly relevant to predicting cooperation rates rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in agent-based models of spatial prisoner's dilemma games with asymmetric punishment, increasing punishment strength or effectiveness increases cooperation rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that, in similar environments, enabling or strengthening punishment will likely increase cooperation rates, which may be correlated with higher efficiency, but this is not directly tested. The paper also shows that the effect of punishment depends on the temptation to defect and the cost-to-fine ratio. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful for understanding how punishment design (asymmetry, cost-to-fine ratio) affects cooperation rates, with only indirect implications for efficiency.

- source: Wen, Y. J., He, Z. X., Shen, C., & Tanimoto, J. (2025). Indirect punishment can outperform direct punishment in promoting cooperation in structured populations. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that, in structured populations modeled as a square lattice, indirect punishment (punishing defectors who harm one's neighbors) can outperform direct punishment (punishing one's own defectors) in promoting cooperation, but only when both the cost and fine of punishment are low. Outside this narrow parameter region, direct punishment may be more effective, both may be equally effective, or neither may improve cooperation. The mechanism is that indirect punishment can impose more total fines on defectors without greatly increasing the cost to punishers, but only when punishment is cheap. The main reported outcomes are cooperation rates and the distribution of strategies; group efficiency or total payoff is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how the cost and scope of punishment affect cooperation rates in spatial social dilemmas. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that indirect punishment can be more effective at promoting cooperation than direct punishment under low-cost, low-fine conditions, but not otherwise. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only adjacent, as the main outcomes are behavioral. The findings may inform expectations about when punishment is likely to increase efficiency, but direct quantitative mapping is not possible from this paper alone.

- source: Vukov, J., Pinheiro, F. L., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2013). Reward from Punishment Does Not Emerge at All Costs. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in structured (scale-free network) populations, the introduction of peer punishment does not always promote cooperation. When the cost-to-impact ratio (q/p) is high, punishment can actually reduce the prevalence of cooperation compared to the no-punishment baseline. Only when the cost of punishing is sufficiently low relative to its impact does punishment enhance cooperation. The effect is mapped across a range of game parameters (T, S) and punishment parameters (p, q), showing regions where punishment is beneficial or detrimental for cooperation. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, but shows that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, on efficiency in adjacent games) is highly sensitive to the cost-effectiveness of punishment (q/p) and to the network structure. In structured populations, punishment can backfire unless costs are low. For prediction, this suggests that the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-like games may be negative or mixed if punishment is costly relative to its impact, especially in networked or heterogeneous settings. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff outcomes limits the direct applicability of these findings to the prediction task.

  findings: The study finds that punishment can promote cooperation in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma with reinforcement learning agents, but only if the punishment is sufficiently strong and not too costly. If the cost of punishment is high or the punishment intensity is low, introducing punishment can actually reduce cooperation below the baseline with no punishment. The system can exhibit both continuous and discontinuous transitions in cooperation as punishment parameters are varied. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators; group efficiency or total payoff is not reported. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence about how punishment parameters (cost, intensity, probability) affect cooperation rates in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma with reinforcement learning. While the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, the results suggest that punishment does not always improve group outcomes: if punishment is too costly or too weak, it can reduce cooperation. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of punishment on efficiency may be non-monotonic and sensitive to cost-effectiveness. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings should be used as qualitative, mechanistic guidance rather than quantitative prediction.

- source: Zhu, W. Q., Pan, Q. H., Song, S., & He, M. F. (2023). Effects of exposure-based reward and punishment on the evolution of cooperation in prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.

  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in an infinite well-mixed population, exposure-based punishment (and especially the combination of reward and punishment) can promote the evolution of cooperation, but only if the cost of exposure is less than the punishment for defection. Rewarding exposed cooperators alone is insufficient to promote cooperation. There are threshold and nonlinear effects: simultaneous reward and punishment is most effective, and there are optimal values for exposure cost and reward/punishment strength. The model identifies parameter regions where cooperation can be sustained, but all results are in terms of the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment (and especially combined with reward) can increase cooperation rates in a prisoner's dilemma setting with exposure-based mechanisms. However, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The model is adjacent to PGGs and focuses on cooperation rates, so its main value is as indirect support for the idea that punishment can promote cooperation under certain cost/benefit conditions. It does not provide quantitative predictions for efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Niu, X. T., Xu, J. W., & Deng, Z. H. (2017). Conditional punishment resolves social dilemma in spatial network. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that introducing and increasing the probability of social punishment (punishers who pay a cost to penalize defectors) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma model leads to a higher steady-state density of cooperators. When the willingness to punish is high, defectors are eliminated and cooperation dominates. The mechanism is shown to be robust across a range of temptation-to-defect values. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates and spatial patterns).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment increases cooperation rates in spatial prisoner's dilemma settings, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation rates may imply higher efficiency, but the cost of punishment and its effect on total group payoff are not analyzed. The model is also not a public goods game, but an adjacent two-player game with spatial structure. Use this paper as indirect support for the behavioral effect of punishment, but not for quantitative efficiency predictions.

- source: Zhang, Y. J., Zeng, Z. Y., Pi, B., & Feng, M. Y. (2023). An evolutionary game with revengers and sufferers on complex networks. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a revenge-based costly punishment mechanism in the PDG on networks increases the frequency of cooperation, especially when the cost to the punisher is low and the cost to the punished is high. The effect is robust across network types (square lattice and small-world), cost-to-benefit ratios, and network sizes. However, the main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff. The model does not report on group welfare, surplus, or total earnings, so direct implications for efficiency are not available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment mechanisms (specifically, revenge-based costly punishment) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma games on networks. However, because the main outcome is the frequency of cooperation and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The model structure and parameterization may inform mechanistic expectations, but quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes is not possible from this paper alone.

- source: Zhang, M., Zhang, X., Qu, C. Q., Wang, G. H., & Lu, X. (2022). The combination of social reward and punishment is conducive to the cooperation and heterogeneity of social relations. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that a combined reward-punishment mechanism (RPM) in a dynamic network prisoner's dilemma can significantly increase the average frequency of cooperation compared to either pure punishment or pure reward alone. Pure punishment only promotes cooperation under high temptation to defect, while pure reward can actually undermine cooperation. The combination of both, with appropriately tuned intensities, sustains high cooperation even under strong temptation to defect. The model also shows that the RPM leads to spontaneous emergence of network heterogeneity, with cooperators becoming influential hubs. However, the main outcome is cooperation frequency, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence and calibration of both punishment and reward mechanisms can strongly affect cooperation rates in dynamic networked social dilemmas. However, because the main outcome is cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a dynamic network prisoner's dilemma rather than a standard public goods game, the findings should be transferred to efficiency prediction with caution. The results suggest that in environments where both punishment and reward are available and appropriately balanced, cooperation (and likely efficiency) can be sustained at higher levels than with either mechanism alone, especially under high temptation to defect. The paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Wang, J., Liu, Z., Xu, Y., & Li, X. P. (2025). Dynamic evolution in multi-player networked trust games with graded punishment. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a graded punishment mechanism in a networked trust game simulation can effectively reduce untrustworthy behavior and increase the density of investment and trust. The effect of punishment depends on both the cost to punishers and the severity of punishment. Graded punishment outperforms traditional punishment in eliminating untrustworthy agents and increasing simulated group trust and total wealth. However, if punishment costs are too high, investment and trust collapse. The results are sensitive to network topology, with regular lattices supporting higher total wealth than scale-free networks. All findings are based on agent-based simulations, not real-world or laboratory experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theory-based evidence that graded punishment mechanisms can increase simulated group trust and total wealth in a networked trust game, especially when punishment is not too costly. However, because the study is simulation-based and uses a trust game variant (not a standard PGG), its findings should be interpreted as suggestive rather than directly predictive for real-world or experimental PGGs. The results highlight the importance of the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and suggest that graded (rather than uniform) punishment may be more effective at sustaining cooperation and group payoff in networked environments. The paper does not provide empirical data for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, but its modeling results may inform hypotheses about the role of punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and network structure.

- source: Li, X., & Cao, L. (2009). Largest Laplacian eigenvalue predicts the emergence of costly punishment in the evolutionary ultimatum game on networks. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in the evolutionary ultimatum game on networks, there is a critical value of the population's selfishness degree (the share of the resource claimed by the proposer) above which a fraction of players become pure costly punishers (always reject offers). This critical value is analytically predicted by the inverse of the largest Laplacian eigenvalue of the network. The emergence of costly punishment is thus determined by network topology. The study also shows that pure punishers can have a higher average payoff ratio than others, especially as selfishness increases, but group-level efficiency or welfare is not directly analyzed. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulations on various network types (scale-free, small-world, real-world collaboration networks).
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants, as it studies the ultimatum game (an adjacent but distinct paradigm) and focuses on the emergence of costly punishment rather than on group efficiency or total payoff. The main outcome is the fraction of pure punishers as a function of network structure and selfishness degree. While the paper provides insight into how network topology affects the threshold for costly punishment, it does not provide data or analysis on how punishment affects group efficiency or welfare in a public-goods-like environment. Thus, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited to theoretical context and mechanism discussion.

- source: Liu, J. Q., & Zhang, Q. W. (2025). Evolutionary dynamics of a probabilistic punishment mechanism with environmental feedback in regular networked Prisoner's Dilemma. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that a probabilistic punishment mechanism with environmental feedback can lead to three types of system states: (1) stable full defection with low probability of effective punishment, (2) stable full cooperation with high probability of effective punishment, and (3) bi-stability where both outcomes are possible depending on initial conditions. Increasing the speed of environmental feedback (i.e., how quickly the probability of effective punishment updates in response to behavior) can shift the system from defection to cooperation in the bi-stable regime. The model highlights the importance of punishment effectiveness, punishment cost, network degree (number of neighbors), and feedback speed in shaping cooperation dynamics. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how probabilistic punishment and environmental feedback affect cooperation in a networked Prisoner's Dilemma, which is adjacent to public goods games. It suggests that punishment mechanisms with high effectiveness and fast feedback can promote cooperation, especially in structured populations. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation frequency and not efficiency or group payoff, the paper offers only indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-like games. The findings may inform the expected direction of efficiency changes (via increased cooperation), but quantitative predictions for efficiency require caution.

- source: Li, B. Q., Wu, Z. X., & Guan, J. Y. (2022). Critical thresholds of benefit distribution in an extended snowdrift game model. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In both infinite and finite structured populations, the critical proportion of benefits that defectors can exploit (while still maintaining stable cooperation) increases with the total resource available, but saturates at a limit. (2) Introducing incentives for cooperators and punishments for defectors increases this critical threshold, meaning that inequality (the share defectors can take) can become worse with such mechanisms. (3) In more highly connected populations, the critical threshold is lower, so inequality is less severe. (4) There is a critical value of incentive/punishment magnitude above which the relationship between resource size and critical threshold reverses. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but rather the conditions for stable coexistence of cooperation and defection.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of punishment (and reward) on the sustainability of cooperation under unequal benefit distributions in a snowdrift game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It suggests that introducing punishment (and reward) can increase the threshold of tolerated inequality, potentially making outcomes less fair for cooperators. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting that punishment can have counterintuitive effects on the distribution of benefits and the persistence of cooperation, especially under varying network structures and resource levels.

- source: Nuño, J. C., Herrero, M. A., & Primicerio, M. (2010). Fighting cheaters: How and how much to invest. *European Journal of Applied Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that in the absence of anti-cheating expenditure, cheaters dominate and total wealth collapses. Introducing and increasing expenditure to fight cheating (via social or punitive means) reduces the cheater population and increases total wealth, but only up to an optimal point. Beyond this, further expenditure reduces wealth due to the cost of enforcement. The optimal strategy involves a balance between social promotion and deterrence, with the best compromise (for the parameter values used) being to spend about 5% of wealth on anti-cheating measures, allocating about 90% of that to social promotion. The model also shows that the effectiveness of punishment (police efficiency) is a critical moderator: if punishment is highly effective, more resources should be allocated to it; if not, social measures are preferable. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values and are supported by sensitivity analyses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that introducing punishment or anti-cheating measures in a public-goods-like environment increases efficiency (total wealth) up to an optimal point, after which further increases in punishment expenditure can reduce efficiency. The model highlights the importance of both the amount spent and the effectiveness of punishment. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment (or anti-cheating expenditure) will increase efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline, but the magnitude depends on the cost, effectiveness, and allocation of resources. The model also warns that excessive punishment spending can be counterproductive. While the model is not an experimental PGG, its structure and outcomes are closely analogous and informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Li, Q., Pi, B., Feng, M. Y., & Kurths, J. (2024). Open Data in the Digital Economy: An Evolutionary Game Theory Perspective. *Ieee Transactions on Computational Social Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and magnitude of regulatory rewards/penalties (F) can shift the system toward cooperative equilibria, where all parties choose the socially desirable strategies (high-quality data provision, data acquisition, and regulation). The model provides explicit conditions under which full cooperation, partial cooperation, or defection equilibria are stable. Increasing the effectiveness or magnitude of regulatory punishment/reward expands the parameter space in which cooperation is stable. However, the main reported outcomes are the equilibrium strategy fractions, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are useful for understanding how punishment/reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in multi-stakeholder environments similar to public goods games, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that increasing the strength or effectiveness of regulatory punishment/reward (analogous to punishment_tech and punishment_cost) can promote cooperative behavior among agents in a multi-stakeholder environment. While the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is informative for predicting that stronger, more effective punishment mechanisms are likely to increase cooperation rates.
