However, because efficiency is not directly analyzed, the paper should be used as indirect evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency, with caution regarding transfer to standard PGG settings.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2022). Advanced defensive cooperators promote cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing advanced defensive cooperators, who can identify and punish anti-defensive defectors at a cost, leads to higher equilibrium fractions of cooperators compared to the standard prisoner's dilemma. The presence of advanced defensive cooperators is especially important when the temptation to defect is high. The model shows that the effectiveness of advanced defensive cooperation depends on parameters such as the temptation value, defensive benefit, defensive loss, fine (punishment), and screening cost. While the model tracks payoffs in the replicator equations, the main reported results are the fractions of each strategy at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (here, advanced defensive cooperation with screening and fines) can increase the prevalence of cooperation in social dilemma settings. However, because the main outcomes are strategy fractions rather than group efficiency or total payoff, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task of forecasting efficiency. The model is a prisoner's dilemma variant, not a public goods game, and does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff. Thus, while the findings suggest that enabling punishment can increase cooperation, they do not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency changes in public-goods-game-like environments.

- source: Jin, Q., Wang, Z., Wang, Z., & Wang, Y. L. (2012). Strategy changing penalty promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a strategy changing penalty (a form of endogenous punishment for switching strategies) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game increases the fraction of cooperators. Both higher penalty rates and longer memory (the period after which the penalty is lifted if the player does not change strategy again) monotonically promote cooperation, with the effect saturating at high memory. The mechanism works by inducing heterogeneity in fitness coefficients, which helps cooperator clusters survive and expand. The paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so the findings are about cooperation rates, not efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that endogenous penalty mechanisms can increase cooperation in spatial social dilemmas, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff. The game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and the punishment is not peer-to-peer but is an automatic penalty for changing strategy. Thus, while the findings suggest that certain forms of punishment can promote cooperation, they do not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The main value is as adjacent, mechanistic support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms can increase cooperation, especially when combined with memory or forgiveness.

- source: Bi, Y., & Yang, H. (2023). Based on reputation consistent strategy times promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner?s dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a reputation-based warning/punishment mechanism—where players are less likely to imitate neighbors who have persistently defected—substantially increases the fraction of cooperators in a spatial PDG simulation. Stronger punishment (higher discount factor) and more timely punishment (lower threshold for triggering the discount) both further increase cooperation rates. The effect is robust across a range of temptation-to-defect parameters. However, the paper does not report on group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment-like mechanisms targeting persistent defectors can increase cooperation rates in spatial PDG environments. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding mechanisms that increase cooperation, but should be used with caution for efficiency prediction, as higher cooperation does not always translate to higher group payoff depending on the cost structure of punishment and the payoff matrix.

- source: Yang, Z. L., Shi, Y. Y., & Li, Y. C. (2018). Analysis of intellectual property cooperation behavior and its simulation under two types of scenarios using evolutionary game theory. *Computers & Industrial Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that under both market and administrative supervision mechanisms, certain policy levers (subsidies, liquidated damages, tax rates, penalties) can increase the probability and stability of cooperation among heterogeneous agents in intellectual property activities. However, the effect is non-monotonic: excessive penalties or taxes can reduce cooperation. The main findings are about the conditions under which the system converges to full cooperation, noncooperation, or mixed states, rather than about efficiency or total group payoff. The results are based on theoretical modeling and numerical simulation, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for how punishment-like mechanisms (liquidated damages, administrative penalties) and incentives (subsidies, tax breaks) affect the evolution of cooperation in a multi-agent setting. While the setting is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured, the results suggest that moderate punishment and incentives can stabilize cooperation, but excessive punishment or taxation can backfire. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the design and magnitude of punishment/reward mechanisms are important moderators of cooperation, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Glover, J., & Kim, E. (2021). Optimal Team Composition: Diversity to Foster Implicit Team Incentives. *Management Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated team production, the efficiency of implicit team incentives (mutual monitoring and punishment for free-riding) depends critically on team composition and the expected duration of the relationship (discount factor). Diverse teams (with productive complementarity) always have a monotonic relationship between tenure and incentive efficiency: as tenure increases, the cost of providing incentives decreases, and collusion is not a concern. Specialized teams (with productive substitutability) can initially benefit from longer tenure (lower incentive costs), but as tenure increases further, collusion becomes a problem, raising the cost of incentives and reducing efficiency. The model provides explicit formulas and parameter thresholds for when each team type is optimal. The main efficiency outcome is the principal's expected payoff, which is maximized by choosing the team composition that best balances productive efficiency and incentive efficiency, given the risk of collusion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms (mutual monitoring, implicit threats) in repeated team production environments, especially as a function of team composition (diversity vs. specialization), the discount factor (tenure), and the production technology (complementarity vs. substitutability). While not a public goods game per se, the model is adjacent and highly relevant for settings where group payoff depends on repeated cooperation and the threat of future punishment. The results show that the efficiency effect of punishment depends on whether collusion is possible and on the structure of the team. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling punishment (mutual monitoring) generally increases efficiency in diverse teams and in specialized teams with short tenure, but may reduce efficiency in specialized teams with long tenure due to collusion. The explicit formulas and parameter thresholds can inform quantitative predictions if the relevant parameters are known.

- source: Frean, M. R., & Abraham, E. R. (2004). Adaptation and enslavement in endosymbiont-host associations - art. no. 051913. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The main finding is that in a two-species repeated cooperation game with differential adaptation rates, the slower-adapting species (host) evolves to provide just enough cooperation to keep the faster-adapting species (symbiont) maximally cooperative. This 'miser-slave' dynamic leads to highly asymmetric payoffs: hosts receive nearly all the benefit, while symbionts receive very little, even though they are highly cooperative. The total group payoff (efficiency) is much less than the maximum possible (full mutual cooperation), because the host withholds cooperation. The model provides a theoretical explanation for observed asymmetries in natural symbioses, where hosts benefit much more than symbionts. The findings are robust across a range of cost-benefit ratios and are supported by both analytic and simulation results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical mechanism showing that, in repeated cooperation games with contingent strategies and differential adaptation rates, the introduction of a contingent response (analogous to punishment or reward) by one party can lead to highly asymmetric efficiency outcomes. Specifically, if the 'punishing' party adapts more slowly, it can extract maximal cooperation from the other party while contributing minimally itself, resulting in low overall efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the structure of contingent responses and adaptation rates can strongly moderate the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms. However, the model is not a standard public goods game, and the 'punishment' is implemented as a contingent reward/withholding rather than explicit costly punishment. Thus, while the findings are informative about the potential for punishment/contingency to reduce efficiency via asymmetry, direct quantitative predictions for PGGs with explicit punishment should be made with caution.

- source: Robert, J. M., Otrok, H., Quttoum, A. N., & Boukhris, R. (2012). A distributed resource management model for Virtual Private Networks: Tit-for-Tat strategies. *Computer Networks*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: Simulation results show that classical Tit-for-Tat fails in this asynchronous, stochastic setting, leading to deadlocks and low total gain. Generous Tit-for-Tat avoids deadlocks and achieves high group gain, but is vulnerable to exploitation by selfish participants. Altruistic and reputation-based strategies can effectively punish selfish participants, forcing them to cooperate to receive benefits. The best strategies (reputation-based and altruistic-reputation) allow cooperative participants to achieve near-optimal total gain, while selfish participants are excluded unless they match cooperative behavior. The findings are robust across variations in the number of cooperative/selfish participants and the probability of offering resources.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that punishment-like mechanisms (retaliation, exclusion, reputation-based refusal) can substantially increase group efficiency in repeated resource-sharing games with asynchronous moves and stochastic cooperation. The results are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment or exclusion in public-goods-like environments, especially when cooperation is probabilistic and not always feasible. The specific game structure differs from standard PGGs (asynchronous, resource lending/borrowing, not simultaneous contribution), but the payoff logic and the effect of punishment on group efficiency are closely analogous. The paper supports the prediction that enabling effective punishment or exclusion will increase efficiency, especially when selfish behavior is otherwise possible.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2024). The universal probabilistic reward based on the difference of payoff realizes the evolution of cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a universal probabilistic reward mechanism, where players with higher payoffs reward their neighbors (cooperators or defectors) with a probability proportional to the payoff difference, leads to a significant increase in both cooperation and average group payoff (efficiency) in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. The effect is robust across different network topologies (regular, random, scale-free) and a range of reward coefficients (t), except when t=0 or t=1, where the mechanism is inactive. The study provides detailed analysis of how the speed and stability of cooperation and efficiency depend on the reward parameter and network structure. No punishment mechanism is implemented in the model, but the paper situates its contribution in the context of prior work on both reward and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that a universal, payoff-difference-based reward mechanism can substantially increase group efficiency in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, even when rewards are given to both cooperators and defectors. While the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, it is highly informative for predicting the effects of reward-based interventions on efficiency, especially in networked or spatially structured games. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, well-designed reward mechanisms can achieve high efficiency. However, the results may not directly transfer to public goods games with explicit punishment options, as the model does not include a punishment treatment or control. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that reward mechanisms (especially those not limited to cooperators) can be highly effective at increasing efficiency, but does not provide direct evidence about the marginal effect of adding punishment to a baseline.

- source: Al-Dhanhani, A., Mizouni, R., Otrok, H., & Al-Rubaie, A. (2014). A game theoretical model for collaborative groups in social applications. *Expert Systems with Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of reciprocity and punishment strategies in repeated social dilemmas (modeled as asynchronous iterated prisoner's dilemma) has a strong effect on group efficiency and the distribution of gains between cooperative and selfish (free riding) users. Classical Tit for Tat leads to deadlock and declining payoffs. Generous Tit for Tat avoids deadlock and increases group gain, but allows free riders to benefit disproportionately. Reputation-based Tit for Tat reduces free rider gain but can also reduce cooperative user gain, especially if the reputation threshold is set high or the initialization phase is short. Group reputation-based Tit for Tat (indirect reciprocity) achieves the highest cooperative user gain and lowest free rider gain, thus maximizing group efficiency and survivability. The presence of free riders always reduces group efficiency, but the right punishment/reciprocity mechanism can mitigate this loss.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of punishment/reciprocity mechanisms (especially those based on group reputation and indirect reciprocity) can significantly improve group efficiency in repeated social dilemma environments, even in the presence of free riders. While the model is not a standard public goods game, it is closely related and the efficiency outcomes are directly comparable. The findings suggest that enabling punishment or exclusion based on group-level reputation (rather than only direct reciprocity) is likely to increase efficiency relative to no punishment or to simple direct reciprocity. The results are most relevant for settings with repeated interactions, observable histories, and the ability to track and act on group-level reputation. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or direct mappings to all design dimensions, but the qualitative direction is clear: more sophisticated, group-based punishment/reciprocity mechanisms are more effective at sustaining efficiency.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2023). Grouping promotes both partnership and rivalry with long memory in direct reciprocity. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The main finding is that group structure, when combined with the availability of longer-memory strategies (which allow for friendly rival strategies), leads to the evolution of nearly full cooperation and maximal efficiency in the repeated donation game. In well-mixed populations, longer memory does not have this effect. The results are robust to the benefit-to-cost ratio and to details of the evolutionary process. The paper does not study public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms, but the repeated donation game is adjacent to PGGs, and friendly rival strategies function as a form of endogenous sanctioning (never allowing the co-player to earn more). Efficiency is measured as the fraction of time mutual cooperation is achieved, which is equivalent to group payoff relative to the maximum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in two-player repeated donation games (adjacent to PGGs), the combination of group structure and the availability of longer-memory strategies (enabling friendly rival strategies) can dramatically increase efficiency, even when cooperation would otherwise be difficult. While there is no explicit punishment mechanism, friendly rival strategies act as endogenous enforcers of cooperation by never allowing the co-player to earn more. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in environments with group structure and sufficient strategic complexity, efficiency can be much higher than in well-mixed or memory-limited settings, even without explicit punishment. However, the results may not transfer directly to multi-player PGGs or to settings with explicit costly punishment.

- source: Zimmermann, M. G., & Eguíluz, V. M. (2005). Cooperation, social networks, and the emergence of leadership in a prisoner's dilemma with adaptive local interactions - art. no. 056118. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that network adaptation (agents breaking unsatisfactory links and seeking new partners) enables the emergence of highly cooperative, high-efficiency steady states in a networked Prisoner's Dilemma. In these states, most agents cooperate, and the group achieves high average payoffs, even though defectors individually do better than cooperators. The system is robust to a range of parameters, but can be destabilized by noise or by the loss of key cooperative leaders, leading to cascades of defection and efficiency collapse. The model does not include explicit costly punishment or reward mechanisms, but the ability to break links with defectors functions as a form of social sanction/ostracism. The results show that such adaptive partner selection can substitute for explicit punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that adaptive partner selection (ostracism, link breaking) in networked social dilemmas can dramatically increase group efficiency, even in the absence of explicit costly punishment or reward. For the downstream prediction task, the findings suggest that enabling mechanisms for social exclusion or network adaptation (even if not labeled as 'punishment') can have similar or greater positive effects on efficiency as explicit punishment in public goods games. However, the model is a Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, and the 'punishment' is indirect (ostracism, not costly reduction of others' payoffs). The results are most relevant for predicting the effects of partner choice, ostracism, or network adaptation, and less directly for classic costly punishment. The paper is highly informative for adjacent mechanisms but should be used with caution for direct predictions about explicit punishment in PGGs.

- source: Iwamura, Y., Nagashima, K., & Tanimoto, J. (2020). Evolutionary dynamics of a 3-strategy game: Cooperator, defector and costly cooperative loner strategic types. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The introduction of a costly cooperative loner (CL) as a third strategy in a prisoner's dilemma game does not prevent the collapse to all-defection, nor does it improve social average payoff (efficiency) compared to the standard two-strategy game. The CL strategy is always outcompeted and goes extinct, after which defectors dominate. This is in contrast to models with costly punishment, where punishment can sometimes sustain cooperation and higher efficiency. The paper provides a detailed analysis of the payoff matrix and evolutionary trajectories, showing that the addition of CL does not alter the efficiency outcome: the system always ends in the least efficient (all-defector) state.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a negative case: simply adding a third strategy that is not an active punisher (but pays a cost to avoid defectors) does not improve efficiency in a prisoner's dilemma environment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of a 'costly loner' or similar non-punishing third strategy is not sufficient to increase efficiency. The results reinforce that only certain types of third strategies (e.g., active costly punishment or rewarding) can shift efficiency outcomes. The findings are most relevant for infinite, well-mixed populations and may not generalize to finite or structured populations. The paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment per se, but its comparison to punishment models supports the view that punishment (with sufficient impact and cost structure) is necessary for efficiency gains in these settings.

- source: Salahshour, M., & Couzin, I. D. (2025). Evolution of altruistic rationality provides a solution to social dilemmas via rational reciprocity. *Physical Review Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary models where agents can evolve their degree of other-regarding preferences (altruism), populations transition from mutual defection (low efficiency) to high cooperation (high efficiency) in social dilemmas like the Prisoner's Dilemma, provided the benefit of cooperation is sufficiently high. This transition is robust to population structure and does not require explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. Instead, 'punishment' of selfishness occurs via the rational withholding of cooperation by conditional cooperators. The resulting group efficiency (average payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) increases substantially compared to the Nash equilibrium of selfish agents. The findings generalize across different two-person two-strategy games, with the mechanism being most pronounced in the Prisoner's Dilemma and also effective in the Snowdrift and Stag Hunt games.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that allowing for the evolution of other-regarding preferences (subjective rationality) can lead to high efficiency in social dilemmas, even without explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. In environments structurally similar to two-player Prisoner's Dilemma, Snowdrift, or Stag Hunt games, if agents can recognize and respond to others' types, group efficiency can approach the cooperative optimum. However, the model does not include explicit punishment costs, technologies, or public goods game features such as multi-player interactions, continuous contributions, or explicit sanctioning. Thus, while the findings are highly informative about the potential for endogenous cooperation and efficiency gains, they are only indirectly applicable to standard public goods games with explicit punishment treatments. The main implication is that mechanisms enabling recognition and conditional cooperation can substitute for explicit punishment in promoting efficiency.

- source: Ille, S. (2021). The evolution of sectarianism. *Communications in Nonlinear Science and Numerical Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of coercive (punishment-like) strategies in a repeated bargaining game can lead to either efficient egalitarian outcomes or inefficient sectarian contracts, depending on the relative costs and benefits of coercion and the structure of social learning. When the cost of coercion is low and potential gains from inegalitarian contracts are high, societies are more likely to settle into inefficient, conflictual, or sectarian equilibria. When both groups have similar coercive capacities and the cost of coercion is high, egalitarian and efficient outcomes are more likely. The model also shows that collective memory, identity politics, and initial asymmetries can tip the balance toward persistent conflict or sectarianism, reducing overall efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (coercion) on efficiency is highly context-dependent. If punishment is symmetric and costly, it can support efficient cooperation (egalitarian contracts). If punishment is asymmetric or the gains from inegalitarian outcomes are high, punishment can entrench inefficient, conflictual, or exploitative equilibria. The model suggests that game design dimensions such as the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the symmetry of player power, and the structure of social learning critically moderate the efficiency impact of punishment. However, the model is not a direct PGG or public goods game, but rather a repeated bargaining/coordination game with endogenous punishment-like coercion. Thus, its findings are most relevant for adjacent settings where punishment can be used to enforce either efficient or inefficient group outcomes.

- source: Zhang, J. L., Zhu, Y. Y., & Chen, Z. Q. (2020). Evolutionary Game Dynamics of Multiagent Systems on Multiple Community Networks. *Ieee Transactions on Systems Man Cybernetics-systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of the interaction network (community structure and interaction probabilities) and the payoff matrix jointly determine which strategies dominate and what equilibrium payoffs are achieved. In dominating games (like the prisoner's dilemma), the system converges to full defection or cooperation, mirroring well-mixed results. In coexistence games (like the snowdrift game), the system can reach an interior equilibrium with mixed strategies, provided certain conditions on the interaction matrix are met. In coordination games (like the stag-hunt), multiple stable equilibria are possible, and the final state depends on initial conditions and network structure. The paper provides sufficient conditions for the stability of equilibria and shows that higher payoff strategies tend to dominate. However, it does not analyze the effect of punishment, reward, or other institutional interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how network structure and payoff matrices affect equilibrium efficiency and welfare in multiagent evolutionary games. However, it does not address the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it model public goods games directly. Its results are most useful for understanding baseline efficiency outcomes in structured populations without institutional interventions. It does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from punishment or reward mechanisms, but it does clarify how efficiency depends on game type and network structure in the absence of such mechanisms.

- source: Szabó, G., & Bunth, G. (2018). Social dilemmas in multistrategy evolutionary potential games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that in symmetric potential games, efficiency (measured as average payoff or contentment) is maximized when the symmetric part of the payoff matrix dominates, aligning individual and collective interests. The introduction of antisymmetric (deluding) components can create social dilemmas, leading to inefficient Nash equilibria and lower average payoffs. The likelihood of inefficiency increases with the number of strategies. However, if the antisymmetric component supports the same equilibrium as the symmetric part, it can help maintain efficiency, especially under noise. The paper provides explicit mappings of parameter regions where efficiency is high or low, but does not study punishment or public goods games directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how the structure of payoff matrices in multi-strategy evolutionary games affects efficiency, especially the role of antisymmetric (deluding) components in creating or resolving social dilemmas. While it does not study public goods games or punishment directly, it offers a framework for predicting when efficiency will be high or low based on the alignment of individual and collective incentives. For the downstream prediction task, the results suggest that efficiency is sensitive to the presence of payoff components that create conflicts between individual and group interests, and that the probability of inefficiency increases with the number of available strategies. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment mechanisms or specific public goods game design dimensions.

- source: Pacheco, J. M., Santos, F. C., & Chalub, F. A. C. C. (2006). Stern-judging: A simple, successful norm which promotes cooperation under indirect reciprocity. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that, in a multilevel evolutionary model of indirect reciprocity, the stern-judging norm—where helping good individuals or refusing help to bad individuals is rewarded, and the opposite is punished—emerges as the dominant and most robust social norm. This norm leads to maximized average payoffs for groups, indicating high efficiency in sustaining cooperation. The result is robust to different forms of group selection and inter-tribe conflict. The model suggests that simple, reputation-based punishment mechanisms are sufficient to promote and maintain high levels of cooperation and group welfare, even in the presence of errors and migration.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that simple, reputation-based punishment mechanisms (like stern-judging) can robustly increase group efficiency and cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings. While the model is not a public goods game per se, it is adjacent: it involves costly helping, reputation, and punishment via social norms. The findings suggest that, in environments where indirect reciprocity and reputation are salient, enabling punishment (in the form of social norm enforcement) is likely to increase group efficiency relative to no-punishment baselines. However, the model is abstract and does not manipulate most game design dimensions directly, so its predictive value is strongest for settings with similar indirect reciprocity structure and less for standard PGGs.

- source: Morsky, B., Plotkin, J. B., & Akcay, E. (2024). Indirect reciprocity with Bayesian reasoning and biases. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that Bayesian reasoning about errors in action and observation generally reduces the equilibrium level of cooperation and group payoff in indirect reciprocity games, compared to models where agents do not reason about errors. This effect is robust across most social norms (Shunning, Simple Standing, Staying, Stern Judging), but under the Scoring norm, Bayesian reasoning can actually promote cooperation and support a mixed equilibrium with all strategy types. The impact of Bayesian reasoning is strongest when error rates are low. Optimism bias (overestimating the frequency of good individuals) generally undermines cooperation, while mild pessimism bias can sometimes promote it, but strong biases of either type destroy cooperation. The model provides heatmaps and bifurcation diagrams showing how cooperation and payoffs change with error rates and biases. The findings are relevant for predicting how changes in information processing, error rates, and social norms affect group efficiency in reputation-based cooperation games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting efficiency in public-goods-like games with punishment/reward mechanisms based on reputation. It shows that the cognitive sophistication of agents (Bayesian reasoning about errors) can reduce group efficiency, except under certain social norms (Scoring). The results suggest that, in environments where punishment/reward is reputation-based and subject to errors, enabling more sophisticated reasoning may not always increase efficiency. The effect of punishment (via conditional defection) on efficiency is highly sensitive to the social norm, error rates, and the presence of optimism/pessimism bias. For downstream prediction, the paper supports the view that punishment mechanisms relying on reputation can backfire or be less effective if agents reason about errors, unless the norm is simple (Scoring). The findings are most transferable to settings where punishment is implemented as conditional cooperation/defection based on reputation, rather than direct costly punishment.

- source: Graser, C., Fujiwara-Greve, T., García, J., & van Veelen, M. (2025). Repeated games with partner choice. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing the option to leave one's partner in repeated prisoner's dilemma games (i.e., partner choice) leads to a substantial increase in average cooperation rates across a wide range of benefit-to-cost ratios and continuation probabilities. This is because partner choice enables endogenous assortment: cooperators can avoid defectors by leaving, which stabilizes cooperation. Theoretical analysis and agent-based simulations both show that, with partner choice, the population spends more time in cooperative states and escapes from universal defection more quickly. The paper compares strategies that punish by defecting (grim trigger) to those that punish by leaving, and finds that punishing by leaving is more robust to invasion and more likely to support cooperation. Although the main outcome is cooperation rate, higher cooperation implies higher average payoffs, so efficiency is indirectly improved by partner choice.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that enabling partner choice (the ability to leave and rematch) in repeated prisoner's dilemma games increases cooperation and, by implication, group efficiency/payoff. While the setting is not a public goods game per se, the mechanism is closely related, and the results are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments where partner choice is possible. The paper does not study costly punishment or explicit sanctions, but rather 'punishment' by leaving. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling partner choice should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control with no partner choice, especially in repeated dyadic games. The results are most directly relevant when the game is dyadic, repeated, and partner choice is the main intervention.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., Iwasa, Y., & Nowak, M. A. (2015). Reputation Effects in Public and Private Interactions. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the stability of cooperative strategies in indirect reciprocity depends critically on the observability of private actions (q), the frequency of public interactions (p), and the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c). Honest cooperation (leading to full group efficiency) is only stable when private actions are sufficiently observable and b/c is high. When private actions are rarely observed or b/c is low, only partial cooperation or defection is stable, resulting in lower group efficiency. The model provides explicit analytical thresholds for these transitions. The presence of public observation increases the incentive for cooperation, but if private actions are unobserved, individuals may defect in private, reducing overall efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how observability (analogous to information feedback or monitoring in public goods games) and the benefit-to-cost ratio moderate the effect of reputation-based incentives on group efficiency. While not a direct PGG or punishment study, its results suggest that increasing the probability that actions are observed (or reputationally consequential) increases the likelihood of stable, high-efficiency cooperation. In prediction tasks, similar mechanisms (e.g., transparency, monitoring, or reputation updating) should be expected to increase the efficiency gains from reputation or sanctioning systems, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high. However, if observability is low, the efficiency impact of such systems will be limited.

- source: Zhou, X., Belloum, A., Lees, M. H., van Engers, T., & de Laat, C. (2023). The dynamics of corruption under an optional external supervision service. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of an optional external supervision service can reduce corruption and promote cooperation, but its effectiveness depends on the cost of supervision and the mutation rates of players and enforcers. Lowering the cost of supervision is not always optimal: if the cost is too low, unnecessary supervision may occur, while if it is too high, corruption may persist. The initial fraction of honest enforcers and the relative mutation rates are critical: higher mutation rates for enforcers (relative to players) favor honest enforcement and trusting cooperation. In finite populations, the system may reach stable equilibria or exhibit persistent oscillations in the fractions of cooperators, defectors, and corrupt enforcers. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so direct implications for efficiency are not provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence about how punishment, corruption, and external supervision interact in a social dilemma context with bribery and enforcement. It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff as a function of game design dimensions. However, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment (via external supervision) is highly sensitive to the cost of supervision and the mutation rates of enforcers and players. The model is adjacent to public goods games, but is not a standard PGG, and the main outcomes are behavioral (strategy fractions), not efficiency. Thus, the paper is more useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators of punishment and corruption than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Guo, H., Chu, C., Shen, C., & Shi, L. (2018). Reputation-based coevolution of link weights promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that reputation-based coevolution of link weights in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game promotes cooperation. When link weights are allowed to vary (parameterized by delta), higher heterogeneity in link weights leads to higher levels of cooperation. Punishment is implemented as a reduction in link weight to the lowest-reputation neighbor, and reward as an increase to the highest-reputation neighbor. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. The effect of punishment and reward is to facilitate the formation of cooperative clusters, which resist invasion by defectors. The effect is stronger for larger delta (greater possible link weight variation).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based punishment and reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial social dilemma settings. However, because the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game), and the outcome is cooperation rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games is limited. The findings may be useful for understanding the role of network structure, reputation, and coevolving relationships in promoting cooperation, but do not provide quantitative guidance for efficiency prediction in PGG-like environments.

- source: Li, X. P., Sun, S. W., & Xia, C. Y. (2019). Reputation-based adaptive adjustment of link weight among individuals promotes the cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that allowing link weights between players to adaptively increase or decrease based on reputation (interpreted as a form of social reward/punishment) leads to a substantial increase in the frequency of cooperation in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift games. The effect is stronger as the range of possible link weights (controlled by ε) increases, which increases heterogeneity and supports the formation of cooperative clusters. The findings are robust across both game types. However, the paper does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its direct relevance to predicting efficiency outcomes is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that dynamic, reputation-based social reward and punishment mechanisms (via link weight adjustment) can promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, and because the model is a simulation of spatial PDG/SDG rather than a public goods game, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited and should be considered only as suggestive, not directly predictive.

- source: Yuan, H. R., Meng, X. Z., & Alzahrani, A. K. (2023). Analysis of dynamic evolution process of the N-player division of labor game model. *Mathematical Modelling of Natural Phenomena*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that high toxicity rates (especially against cheaters), high toxin levels, and smaller group sizes promote the disappearance of cheaters and the persistence of cooperation (cooperators and/or toxin producers). The system exhibits bistability: depending on initial conditions and parameters, the population may converge to all cooperators, coexistence of cooperators and toxin producers, or coexistence of cooperators and cheaters. The interior equilibrium (all three strategies coexisting) is always unstable. Bifurcation analysis reveals thresholds in absorption rates and toxin parameters that determine which strategies persist. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how costly sanctioning (here, toxin production) can suppress free riding and promote cooperation in N-player games. However, the model is not a standard public goods game with explicit peer punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome. The findings suggest that increasing the effectiveness or prevalence of costly sanctions (analogous to punishment) can reduce free riding and increase the fraction of task-performers, especially in smaller groups and with higher sanction effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment-like mechanisms can increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes or direct mapping to standard PGG dimensions.

- source: Hu, Q., Jin, T., Jiang, Y. L., & Liu, X. W. (2024). Reputation incentives with public supervision promote cooperation in evolutionary games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that a reputation incentive mechanism with public supervision can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial PDG. The effect is stronger when the evaluation intensity (the range of possible evaluation values) is lower, allowing players to more quickly update their evaluations and reputations. The model demonstrates that reputation-based rewards can compensate cooperators for losses due to defectors, promoting the formation and persistence of cooperative clusters. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based reward mechanisms with public supervision can increase cooperation rates in spatial PDG-like environments, especially when evaluation intensity is low. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs. The findings may be useful for understanding the role of reputation and reward mechanisms as adjacent or complementary to punishment, but should not be used as direct evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Li, G. Y., Du, H. F., & He, X. C. (2024). The evolutionary prisoner's dilemma game in continuous signed networks. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that negative relationships, modeled as punitive ties that reduce payoffs for defectors, can promote the emergence and stability of cooperation in networked prisoner's dilemma games. The effect is stronger when the negative coefficient (punishment strength) or the proportion of negative ties is higher. Adaptive networks, where agents can adjust their ties, further facilitate cooperation. Structural balance (alignment of positive ties within groups and negative ties between groups) also supports higher cooperation. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff, focusing instead on the behavioral outcome of cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment-like mechanisms (here, negative relationships that reduce payoffs) can increase cooperation rates in networked social dilemmas. However, because the main outcome is the proportion of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, and because the model is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The findings suggest that increasing the cost or prevalence of punishment can promote cooperation, but do not quantify the effect on group efficiency or welfare.

- source: Deng, L. L., Zhang, X. X., & Wang, C. (2021). Coevolution of spatial ultimatum game and link weight promotes fairness. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The coevolution of game strategy and link weight in a spatial ultimatum game model leads to higher average proposal and acceptance levels, interpreted as greater fairness. The key driver is the link weight boundary control parameter (\delta), which, when increased, allows for greater heterogeneity in social ties and promotes fairer strategies. The adjustment amplitude (\Delta) has a smaller or context-dependent effect. The model does not analyze or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its direct relevance to efficiency prediction in public goods or similar games is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The model is an ultimatum game (not a PGG or direct variant), and the main outcomes are behavioral (fairness, proposal/acceptance levels) rather than payoff-based. The 'punishment' in this context is the weakening of social ties, not costly punishment of the type used in PGGs. Therefore, while the findings may be conceptually adjacent—suggesting that dynamic social relationships can promote fairer behavior—they do not inform quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Zhang, Y. L., Yang, S., Chen, X. J., Bai, Y. B., & Xie, G. M. (2023). Reputation update of responders efficiently promotes the evolution of fairness in the ultimatum game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that indirect reciprocity mechanisms based on reputation can strongly promote fairness in the ultimatum game. Specifically, updating responders' reputation via image scoring leads to a very high level of fairness (over 95%), while updating proposers' reputation alone increases fairness above the baseline (no reputation) for many social norms, with a few norms achieving over 80% fairness. When both proposers' and responders' reputations are updated, most social norms yield even higher fairness than updating proposers' reputation alone, but never exceed the effect of updating responders' reputation alone. The results are based on evolutionary game-theoretic modeling and analysis of strategy dynamics, not on empirical or experimental data. The main dependent variable is the frequency of fair offers and rejections of unfair offers, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. While it does not study public goods games or efficiency/payoff outcomes directly, it analyzes how indirect reciprocity and reputation mechanisms affect fairness (a behavioral outcome) in the ultimatum game, which is a related but distinct environment. The findings suggest that mechanisms analogous to punishment (here, rejection of unfair offers, i.e., altruistic punishment) can strongly promote norm compliance and fairness, especially when reputation is at stake. However, because the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, and the game is not a public goods game, the results should be used only as weak, indirect evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Vukov, J. (2014). Accurate reactions open up the way for more cooperative societies. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that agents with more accurate and detailed memory (mem-1 strategies) can achieve much higher levels of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma than agents with only reactive memory. This effect is robust across a wide range of temptation-to-defect values, provided that the copying process is accurate (low error/noise). When copying errors increase, cooperation collapses. The paper interprets these results as showing that cognitive sophistication enables more stable and widespread cooperation, which in turn is associated with higher average payoffs, though payoff is not the primary outcome. There is no explicit punishment or sanctioning institution; instead, retaliation and forgiveness are embedded in the strategy space.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only indirectly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not study public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms. Instead, it analyzes how cognitive sophistication in memory and strategy (allowing for retaliation and forgiveness) affects cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The findings suggest that mechanisms enabling accurate, context-sensitive responses (such as retaliation or forgiveness) can promote cooperation and, by implication, higher payoffs, but this is not directly mapped to efficiency or to institutional punishment. The results may be useful for understanding the role of memory, strategy complexity, and error in sustaining cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment interventions on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Khatun, K., Shen, C., Shi, L., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Stability of pairwise social dilemma games: Destructive agents, constructive agents, and their joint effects. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in pairwise social dilemma games, introducing destructive agents (who opt out and harm others) does not promote cooperation, in contrast to their effect in public goods games. Instead, destructive agents destabilize or replace defection (in prisoner's dilemma and stag-hunt) or undermine cooperation (in harmony and chicken games). Constructive agents (who opt out and benefit others) can have similar destabilizing effects if their own payoff is high, but if their payoff is lower than what they give, they mainly destabilize defection without harming cooperation. When both agent types are present, the system exhibits complex multistable equilibria, but cooperation is not promoted. The analysis is entirely theoretical, using replicator dynamics and stability analysis. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not model punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes, and its focus is on pairwise (dyadic) games rather than multiplayer public goods games. The closest relevance is in its discussion of how certain opt-out strategies (destructive/constructive agents) affect the stability of cooperation, but not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper should not be weighted strongly in predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for understanding the limits of voluntary participation and opt-out mechanisms in pairwise games, but not for the core prediction task.

- source: Li, S. L., He, Z. X., Jia, D. Y., Shen, C., Shi, L., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Granting leaders priority exit options promotes and jeopardizes cooperation in social dilemmas. *Neurocomputing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that granting exit rights to high-degree (leader) nodes in a spatial prisoner's dilemma can either suppress or promote cooperation, depending on the strength of the social dilemma and the network's assortativity. In weak dilemmas, leader-priority exit reduces cooperation, while in strong dilemmas, it can promote cooperation by isolating defectors and allowing cooperative clusters to survive among low-degree nodes. Random and fringe-priority exit have weaker or negligible effects. The main outcome is the relative cooperation ratio among non-exiters. The model does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods game. The exit option is not a punishment mechanism per se, but can act as a hard sanction by denying payoffs to others.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma with an exit option, not a PGG, and the main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The exit option is not a punishment mechanism, though it can function as a hard sanction. The findings may be relevant for understanding how voluntary exclusion or exit options affect cooperation in networked dilemmas, but do not provide direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. Use this paper only for very indirect, mechanistic insights about how exclusion or exit options might interact with network structure and dilemma strength.

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that incorporating both direct and indirect experience in interaction and strategy update rules, as well as introducing tolerance in link weight adjustment, significantly promotes cooperation in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma simulations. High sensitivity to defection (low tolerance) and greater weight on indirect experience both increase the frequency of cooperation and the average payoff of cooperators relative to defectors. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total welfare outcomes. The findings are based on simulation results and are not directly transferable to public goods games with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms allowing individuals to avoid defectors (via adaptive link weights and social learning) can increase cooperation and the average payoff of cooperators in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma settings. However, it does not study public goods games, does not implement explicit punishment or sanctioning, and does not report group efficiency or total welfare. Thus, its relevance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is limited and should be considered only as weak, mechanistic context.

- source: Zhang, Y., Hao, Q. Y., Qian, J. L., Wu, C. Y., Guo, N., & Ling, X. (2024). The cooperative evolution in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game with the local loyalty of two-strategy. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.

  findings: The study finds that incorporating local loyalty to both cooperation and defection strategies in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can promote the emergence and stability of cooperation, especially when the strength index of local loyalty is positive and the weighting between payoff and loyalty is balanced. There are optimal parameter values that maximize cooperation frequency, and the system can reach full cooperation under these conditions. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and all results are based on simulation of behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates and loyalty patterns).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. While it explores mechanisms (loyalty, reputation) that are conceptually adjacent to punishment and cooperation, it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings may be contextually informative for understanding how social mechanisms can promote cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence for the efficiency prediction task.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2021). Environmental-based defensive promotes cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that allowing cooperators to take defensive actions (at a cost) in response to environmental cues (number of other cooperators) can prevent the collapse to full defection and promote higher levels of cooperation. The effectiveness of this mechanism depends on the defensive cost, defensive benefit, defensive loss, and the threshold for defensive awareness. When defensive measures are sufficiently beneficial and not too costly, and when cooperators are sufficiently vigilant (high threshold and probability), the system can reach full cooperation or stable coexistence. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical evidence that mechanisms allowing individuals to reduce exploitation (analogous to defensive or sanctioning actions) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemmas. However, because the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game) and reports cooperation fraction rather than efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The results suggest that the effectiveness of defensive/sanctioning mechanisms depends on their cost, benefit, and the conditions under which they are triggered, which may inform the expected direction of punishment effects in related models. However, quantitative predictions for efficiency cannot be extracted from this paper.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2021). Effects of defensive cooperation strategy on the evolution of cooperation in social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The introduction of a defensive cooperation strategy (where cooperators pay a cost to reduce losses against defectors) can promote and sustain higher levels of cooperation in both well-mixed and structured populations, compared to the standard prisoner's dilemma. The effectiveness of this strategy depends on the balance between the defensive cost and defensive benefit. When the defensive benefit exceeds the cost, defensive cooperators can suppress defectors and allow cooperation to persist. However, excessive defensive benefit can lead to second-order free-riding by unconditional cooperators. The model shows cyclic dominance among the three strategies in structured populations. The paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so its findings are not directly translatable to efficiency prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its model is a theoretical extension of the prisoner's dilemma with a defensive cooperation strategy, which is conceptually adjacent to punishment but not equivalent. The outcomes are behavioral (strategy fractions), not payoff-based. Therefore, while the paper suggests that self-interested defensive strategies can promote cooperation under certain conditions, it does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency or group payoff that can be directly used for the downstream prediction task. Its relevance is limited to mechanism discussion and indirect contextualization.

- source: Li, W. J., Jiang, L. L., & Perc, M. (2021). A limited mobility of minorities facilitates cooperation in social dilemmas. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma model, allowing individuals to move away from defectors with low probability (limited mobility) increases the frequency of cooperation by enabling the formation and expansion of cooperative clusters. High mobility, however, undermines cooperation by disrupting network reciprocity. The authors interpret mobility away from defectors as a form of punishment, but the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The main reported outcome is cooperation frequency, not efficiency or group payoff. The paper claims that limited mobility of minorities can promote cooperation more effectively and with less public resource cost than explicit punishment or reward, but this is not empirically tested.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The model is a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma, not a PGG, and the main outcome is cooperation frequency, not efficiency or group payoff. The analogy between mobility/alienation and punishment is discussed, but there is no explicit manipulation of punishment cost, effectiveness, or related design dimensions. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how indirect forms of sanctioning (like alienation) can promote cooperation, but they do not provide quantitative or direct evidence for the prediction task focused on efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

  findings: The paper finds that the probability of enterprises joining a pollution control coalition depends on the relative costs and benefits, the sharing of profits, and the initial willingness to cooperate. Under certain parameter conditions, all enterprises will join the coalition (full cooperation), while under others, none will join. The model shows that the presence of random disturbances (stochasticity) leads to fluctuations in strategies but does not fundamentally alter the stable outcomes. The analysis highlights the importance of effective incentive and punishment mechanisms for coalition stability, but these are discussed at a conceptual level rather than modeled as explicit game interventions. The main findings are about the conditions for stable coalition formation, not about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides only indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. While it discusses the importance of incentive and punishment mechanisms for coalition stability, it does not model or experimentally test the effect of enabling or disabling punishment on group efficiency or payoff. The model is adjacent to PGGs in that it studies cooperation among multiple agents with shared benefits and costs, but the main outcomes are behavioral (coalition formation) rather than efficiency. The paper may inform mechanistic expectations about the role of incentives and punishment in sustaining cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Newton, P. K., & Ma, Y. (2021). Maximizing cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma evolutionary game via optimal control. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that by optimally controlling the off-diagonal elements of the prisoner's dilemma payoff matrix (interpreted as dynamically changing incentives and penalties), it is possible to minimize the defector population (maximize cooperation) over a fixed time horizon. The optimal control is bang-bang and depends on the initial state. The reduction in defectors is most efficient for small initial defector populations. The method cycles through a sequence of different games (PD, Leader, Deadlock, etc.) to achieve the optimal outcome. The approach is adaptive over multiple cycles, recalculating the optimal schedule based on the current state. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and is not based on experimental or empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is a theoretical study of optimal control in a two-population evolutionary prisoner's dilemma, focusing on minimizing defection via dynamic changes to the payoff matrix. There is no explicit punishment mechanism, no group payoff or efficiency outcome, and the setting is not a public goods game. The findings may be conceptually relevant for understanding how dynamic incentives can shape cooperation, but do not provide quantitative or experimental evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Sinatra, R., Iranzo, J., Gómez-Gardeñes, J., Floría, L. M., Latora, V., & Moreno, Y. (2009). The Ultimatum Game in complex networks. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The main findings are that network topology and the type of evolutionary update rule strongly influence the distribution of offers and acceptance thresholds in the Ultimatum Game. Under natural selection, strategies converge toward fair offers (around 50%) in both homogeneous and heterogeneous networks, but scale-free networks allow for a broader range of surviving strategies due to the influence of hubs. Under the social penalty rule, which penalizes the lowest-payoff player and its neighbors, scale-free networks promote polarization: hubs can afford to be highly generous, while low-degree nodes may be more selfish. When players can independently choose offers and thresholds, the population tends to self-organize into pragmatic (role-ignoring) strategies, especially under social penalty. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, so the implications for efficiency are not directly addressed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies the Ultimatum Game and focuses on the evolution of behavioral strategies rather than payoff or efficiency outcomes. The 'social penalty' mechanism is a form of network-level selection rather than direct costly punishment. While the findings may be informative about how network structure and update rules affect the evolution of cooperation and fairness-related behaviors, they do not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs. Therefore, this paper should be considered only as weak, adjacent evidence for the prediction task.
