Depending on payoff parameters and the number of neighbors, the system can exhibit persistent oscillations, bistability (with both depleted and replete environmental states as attractors), coexistence of oscillation and dominance, or stable coexistence of cooperation and defection. The number of neighbors (degree) can either expand or shrink the basin of attraction for favorable environmental states, depending on the ratio of enhancement/degradation rates or payoff parameter ratios. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not manipulate or model costly punishment or sanctions, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The model is adjacent to PGGs in that it studies cooperation and defection in a structured population with environmental feedback, but the 'punishment' parameter is the matrix payoff for mutual defection, not a sanctioning institution. The findings may inform how structural parameters (e.g., number of neighbors) and payoff ratios affect the maintenance of cooperation and environmental quality, but do not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about the effect of punishment interventions on efficiency.

- source: Gong, H. W., Xiang, H. J., Wang, Y. F., Gao, H. J., & Meng, X. Z. (2024). Strategy evolution of a novel cooperative game model induced by reward feedback and a time delay. *Aims Mathematics*.

  findings: The paper finds that in a theoretical evolutionary game with dynamic, feedback-based rewards (but no punishment), the system can exhibit multiple stable equilibria, bistability, and oscillatory dynamics depending on parameters and time delay. Increasing the reward does not always increase cooperation; under some conditions, even maximal rewards do not induce cooperation. The initial state can determine which equilibrium is reached. Introducing time delay can destabilize previously stable equilibria and induce stable limit cycles (oscillations) in the proportion of cooperators and reward intensity. The model does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanctions.

It is a theoretical analysis of a different class of evolutionary games (general 2x2 matrix) with dynamic rewards and time delay, focusing on the evolution of cooperation and reward intensity. There is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and no implementation of punishment or sanctions. The findings may be of limited, indirect interest for understanding how dynamic incentives and time delays affect cooperation dynamics, but they do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Martinez-Vaquero, L. A. (2023). Inequality leads to the evolution of intolerance in reputation-based populations. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that economic inequality is a strong enhancer of intolerance in reputation-based populations. Intolerant strategies can invade tolerant groups more easily when inequality is high, even if overall economic stress is moderate. Redistribution of wealth can prevent or reverse the spread of intolerance, but only if implemented before intolerance becomes widespread. The model shows that tolerant minorities under economic stress may reduce their willingness to punish defectors or may even help wealthy discriminators. The main reported outcomes are the intention to cooperate and the evolutionary stability of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. It suggests that in reputation-based cooperation environments, inequality and group structure can strongly moderate the effectiveness of punishment-like mechanisms (here, reputation-based exclusion) in sustaining cooperation. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings are most useful for understanding how inequality and group structure might interact with punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms beyond the standard PD payoffs.

The findings may be of some indirect relevance for understanding how environmental constraints can promote cooperation, but do not provide evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

Link-type strategists can selectively cooperate or defect with neighbors, effectively punishing defectors and protecting cooperative clusters. - source: Yang, Z. H., & Zhang, L. P. (2023). Random migration with tie retention promotes cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that tie retention (the ability to keep social ties with ex-neighbors after migration) can facilitate the emergence and maintenance of cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game, even when migration is random. The maximum number of retained ex-neighbors (m) is a key parameter: higher m leads to higher cooperation rates. Social diversity in tie retention (variation in m across players) further enhances cooperation. Migration probability (p) has a non-monotonic effect: lower p generally favors cooperation, but diversity in p can create optimal conditions for cooperation. The results challenge the previous view that random migration is always detrimental to cooperation. However, all findings are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or model punishment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its main relevance is in showing that network structure, migration, and tie retention can strongly affect cooperation rates in the prisoner's dilemma game. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that social stickiness (tie retention) and migration patterns are important moderators of cooperation, but it does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency.

  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated RSP game modeling P2P interactions, the introduction of a 'loner' strategy (voluntary withdrawal) allows for stable coexistence of cooperators, non-cooperators, and loners, preventing the collapse of cooperation even in the absence of incentives or punishment. The system exhibits cyclic dominance of strategies, and the stability of cooperation is sensitive to factors such as population size, network overlay dynamism, and initial population composition. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. Simulations show that large, weakly dynamic populations are more likely to sustain stable cycles, while small or highly dynamic overlays are prone to domination by a single strategy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main relevance is in showing that, in a repeated game with voluntary participation (the 'loner' option), stable cooperation can emerge without explicit punishment or reward. The model is adjacent to PGGs but differs in key respects: it is a zero-sum RSP game, not a public goods game, and does not manipulate or analyze punishment, reward, or efficiency. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs, but may be useful for understanding the role of voluntary participation and cyclic dominance in sustaining cooperation.

  findings: The paper finds that introducing quasi-cooperators (who are intermediate between cooperators and defectors) can, under certain parameter regimes, help maintain cooperation by resisting defectors. However, quasi-cooperators also exploit cooperators, reducing their payoff. The evolutionary outcome depends on parameters such as the temptation to defect, the degree of quasi-cooperation, and the cost to defectors. High costs to defectors and moderate levels of quasi-cooperation can promote the coexistence of cooperators and quasi-cooperators, and suppress defectors. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

While it discusses how intermediate strategies (quasi-cooperation) can affect the evolutionary stability of cooperation, it does not model or analyze explicit punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. The findings may be relevant for understanding how intermediate or impure strategies affect cooperation dynamics, but do not provide quantitative or direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The model is adjacent to, but not directly informative for, the prediction task.

- source: Tucker, W. T., & Ferson, S. (2008). Evolved altruism, strong reciprocity, and perception of risk. *Strategies for Risk Communication: Evolution, Evidence, Experience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that human cognitive architecture is shaped by evolutionary pressures to maintain cooperation and punish free riders in social contract networks, such as those modeled by public goods games. It reviews evidence that people are willing to incur personal costs to punish defectors, supporting the concept of 'strong reciprocity.' The authors suggest that these evolved mechanisms are central to risk perception and cooperation, and that they help explain why people often resist narrowly self-interested, rational-maximizing behavior in favor of fairness and contract maintenance. However, the paper does not provide new empirical data or direct analysis of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and contextual support for the idea that punishment mechanisms are evolutionarily grounded and play a key role in sustaining cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It supports the expectation that punishment can increase cooperation and deter free riding, but does not provide quantitative or direct empirical evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment is likely to increase cooperation and potentially efficiency, but does not provide parameter-specific or design-dimension-specific guidance. Its value is mainly in supporting the plausibility and generality of punishment effects, not in calibrating their magnitude or moderators.

- source: Bi, Y., Hao, Q. Y., & Wu, W. J. (2024). The warning effect of persistent defection strategy promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper proposes that extortion, prosocial reciprocity, and punishment are all forms of conditional control strategies that use contingent incentives to shape partner behavior. It argues that intuition and inflexible commitment are key psychological mechanisms that enable these strategies to overcome intertemporal and communicative challenges. The authors review circumstantial evidence from studies of psychopathy, social value orientation, and economic games (e.g., prisoner's dilemma, public goods) to suggest that both prosocial and antisocial (extortionate) strategies can be supported by intuitive processes. However, the paper does not provide new empirical findings or direct evidence on the effects of punishment on efficiency or payoffs in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or related environments. Its main contribution is conceptual: it suggests that the psychological mechanisms supporting punishment and extortion (such as intuition and inflexibility) may be similar, and that both prosocial and antisocial strategies can be implemented intuitively. For the downstream prediction task, this paper may provide useful context for understanding the role of psychological commitment in punishment and extortion, but it does not offer quantitative or experimental evidence about how game design dimensions or control efficiency predict treatment efficiency with punishment enabled.

- source: Si, Z. H., He, Z. X., Shen, C., & Tanimoto, J. (2023). Speculative defectors as unexpected insulators of super cooperators in structured populations. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that the interplay between environmental feedback and game parameters (replenishment rate, dilemma strength, loner payoff, draw payoff) determines the long-run prevalence of cooperation, defection, and abstention in the population. Under resource scarcity, loners dominate; under resource abundance, all strategies coexist in a no-dominance equilibrium (RPS-like). Cyclic dominance emerges under intermediate conditions. The model shows that environmental feedback can shift the system between social dilemma and cyclic dominance regimes. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment/reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main relevance is in illustrating how environmental feedback and the availability of an abstention (loner) strategy can alter the dynamics of cooperation and defection, sometimes leading to cyclic dominance. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not address punishment, efficiency, or the standard PGG structure directly.

- source: Bae, M., Shimada, T., & Baek, S. K. (2024). Exact cluster dynamics of indirect reciprocity in complete graphs. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper provides an exact theoretical analysis of how different indirect reciprocity norms (L4 and L6) shape the long-run social structure in large, fully connected populations. Under L6 (stern judging), the population almost always segregates into two mutually hostile clusters as the group size increases, due to the entropy of balanced configurations. Under L4, a subtle change in the assessment rule leads to a high probability of the 'paradise' state, where everyone assesses everyone else positively, especially as group size increases. The findings are about the stationary distribution of assessment configurations and the emergence of social structure, not about efficiency or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. While it is highly informative about the structural consequences of different indirect reciprocity norms (i.e., whether societies segregate or reach universal cooperation), it does not model or report payoffs, efficiency, or welfare. The results may be relevant for understanding the conditions under which cooperation is structurally stable, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions or control efficiency.

- source: Park, J. (2018). Changes in political party systems arising from conflict and transfer among political parties. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that when both competition (conflict) and transfer (recruitment) rates among three political parties are weak, coexistence of all parties is robust. As either competition or transfer increases, the system transitions to single-party or two-party dominance, with the possibility of bistable or multistable outcomes depending on initial conditions. The maintenance of coexistence is sensitive to the balance between competition and transfer. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not report any payoff or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. Its findings are about the stability of coexistence versus dominance in a theoretical model of party competition and transfer, which is only adjacent to the target domain. It does not manipulate or analyze any of the key game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, MPCR, player count) in a way that maps to efficiency or payoff. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for the prediction task, though it may provide some very indirect conceptual context about the effects of conflict and transfer on group structure.

- source: Lee, H. W., & Weng, Y. N. (2025). Granular Q-learning adaptation boosts collective welfare in multi-agent Prisoner's Dilemma. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that more granular, neighbor-specific Q-learning (ID) enables agents to achieve higher collective welfare (average payoff) and maintain cooperation more robustly than aggregated Q-learning (II) in multi-agent Prisoner's Dilemma games. This advantage is most pronounced at intermediate dilemma strengths and in homogeneous networks, but persists in scale-free networks as well. The results are based on extensive simulations varying network structure, dilemma strength, and agent composition. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present; the only difference is the learning architecture. The findings suggest that the granularity of learning adaptation is a key moderator of efficiency in social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial and scale-free Prisoner's Dilemma environments, the granularity of learning adaptation (neighbor-specific vs. aggregated) is a major determinant of collective efficiency. While not a public goods game or a punishment study, the results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games where the main design dimension is the learning rule or information granularity. The absence of punishment/reward means the findings are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled PGGs, but the payoff-based outcome and the focus on learning architecture offer useful indirect evidence for how cognitive sophistication or information structure can boost efficiency. The results are most applicable to settings where agent adaptation, rather than explicit sanctions, is the main intervention.

- source: Das Bairagya, J., Mondal, S. S., Chowdhury, D., & Chakraborty, S. (2023). Eco-evolutionary games for harvesting self-renewing common resource: effect of growing harvester population. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the realization or prevention of the tragedy of the commons (TOC) in a common-pool resource system depends critically on the relationship between the resource's intrinsic growth rate and the harvesting rates of the population, as well as on the incentive structure encoded in the payoff matrix. When the resource's growth rate is between the low and high harvesting rates, both collapse and partial/complete prevention of TOC are possible, depending on initial conditions and parameter values. Finite population size (carrying capacity) can introduce bistability and limit cycles, making the system's fate sensitive to initial conditions. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, but suggests that such extensions could be studied in future work. The main outcome relevant to efficiency is the long-run resource state, which serves as a proxy for group welfare or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how resource dynamics, harvesting strategies, and population size interact to determine group efficiency in common-pool resource settings. While it does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, it offers a detailed analysis of how efficiency (resource state) depends on game parameters such as harvesting rates, resource growth, and population carrying capacity. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding baseline efficiency outcomes in the absence of punishment or reward, and for highlighting the importance of ecological feedbacks and population size. It does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does suggest that efficiency is highly sensitive to the incentive structure and ecological parameters. The discussion of future extensions to include policing or punishment indicates that the model could be adapted for such analyses.

- source: Hadzibeganovic, T., Cui, P. B., & Wu, Z. X. (2019). Nonconformity of cooperators promotes the emergence of pure altruism in tag-based multi-agent networked systems. *Knowledge-based Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in tag-based multi-agent systems, the combination of spatial structure (small-world networks) and the presence of nonconforming (free) altruists, together with conformity-biased transmission of other strategies, is necessary for the stable dominance of pure altruism (unconditional cooperation). Conformity alone, or spatial structure alone, is insufficient to produce this outcome. The model shows that even a small fraction (>1%) of nonconforming altruists can drive the system toward near-complete altruism, resulting in higher mean fitness and group payoff. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are robust across a range of network parameters and conversion sensitivities. The results are supported by both agent-based simulations and mathematical analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it is highly informative about the role of conformity, spatial structure, and unconditional cooperation in evolutionary games with tag-based interactions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly applicable, but it does provide a strong theoretical and computational baseline for understanding how other mechanisms (conformity, spatial structure, nonconformity) can promote efficiency and cooperation in the absence of punishment. If a model or prediction task involves tag-based cooperation, conformity, or spatial structure, the findings here suggest that these factors can be powerful moderators of efficiency, but the effect of punishment would need to be assessed from other sources.

- source: Li, C., Xu, H. D., & Fan, S. H. (2020). Synergistic effects of self-optimization and imitation rules on the evolution of cooperation in the investor sharing game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the proposed dynamic strategy updating rule can substantially increase the density of cooperators in the investor sharing game, especially at low aspiration levels. The self-optimization rule increases the probability that defectors with high investment become cooperators, which then promotes further imitation of cooperation. Lower market monopoly (parameter alpha) and more random network structure also boost cooperation, but the effect of network randomness is stronger. Average payoffs for cooperators can exceed those for defectors at high cooperation rates, but group efficiency is not directly analyzed. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of punishment effects on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on cooperation rates and average payoffs under different strategy updating rules and aspiration levels may be of indirect interest for understanding how endogenous learning rules affect cooperation, but it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment, nor does it report group efficiency as defined for the prediction task.

- source: Lu, W., & Liang, S. (2025). The role of strategy-affected emotions in the evolution of cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial three-strategy prisoner's dilemma model with emotion dynamics, increasing the intensity of friendliness (the degree to which strategies affect emotions) leads to higher levels of cooperation and higher average payoffs in the population. The effect is robust across different parameterizations of the game (including donor-recipient and boundary PD variants). Memory length moderates the effect: with low friendliness, longer memory increases cooperation; with high friendliness, longer memory decreases cooperation. The model demonstrates that stable, high-payoff cooperation can emerge through emotion-strategy feedbacks alone, without any punishment or reward mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It is relevant as a theoretical demonstration that high cooperation and group payoff can be achieved through emotion-based mechanisms, without punishment or reward. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects, this paper suggests that alternative mechanisms (like friendliness) can substitute for punishment in promoting efficiency, but it does not inform how punishment would change efficiency relative to a control. The game design dimensions relevant to punishment (punishment_cost, punishment_tech, etc.) are not present or manipulated. Thus, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in the absence of punishment or reward.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that asymmetric rewards in a two-population prisoner's dilemma model can sustain cooperation and prevent total defection, especially in structured populations (lattices). The effect of asymmetry on cooperation depends on the dilemma strength: under strong dilemma (high temptation to defect), asymmetry promotes cooperation; under weak dilemma, strong asymmetry can actually inhibit cooperation. The model includes both prosocial and antisocial rewards but does not include punishment or sanctions. The outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates and the coexistence of different strategies, not in terms of efficiency or group payoff.

Its findings on asymmetric rewards in the prisoner's dilemma may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how positive incentives (rewards) can sustain cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment or efficiency. The game structure, outcome measures, and intervention type (reward, not punishment) are all different from the target prediction context.

  findings: The study finds that certain conditional cooperation strategies, especially those tolerant of cooperative behavior and harsh toward defection (the 'catalyst strategy'), can promote high levels of cooperation in indirect reciprocity models based on second-order action labels. The presence of noise (action or label errors) reduces cooperation rates but does not qualitatively change the system's state. Larger populations enhance the effectiveness of the catalyst strategy, while smaller populations and higher noise diminish it. However, the model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and efficiency or group payoff is not a primary outcome.

  findings: The paper finds that introducing multiple interaction patterns (conditional and stochastic) based on opponent's ability can significantly increase the density of cooperators in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation density, not efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Li, S. L., Hong, L. J., Geng, Y. N., & Shen, C. (2020). Popularity-driven fitness calculation promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that integrating popularity into the fitness function (via a tunable parameter alpha) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can significantly promote cooperation. When alpha is positive, popular individuals (those whose neighbors share their strategy) gain higher fitness, leading to the formation and expansion of cooperative clusters and, in some cases, full cooperation. When alpha is negative, isolated cooperators can persist but do not dominate. The results are supported by both numerical simulations and mean-field theoretical analysis. However, the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, nor does it include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that mechanisms which enhance the fitness of popular strategies can promote cooperation, but this is not directly transferable to the efficiency effects of punishment. The game studied is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and the outcome of interest is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency.

However, the paper does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, and does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.

  findings: The main finding is that PSO-based strategy updating rules can enhance cooperation in continuous strategy systems (where players can choose any cooperation level between 0 and 1), but this effect disappears in mixed strategy systems (where players must choose all-or-nothing cooperation or defection). The weighting parameter ω, which balances historical best and neighbor imitation, has a non-monotonic effect: low ω promotes cooperation when temptation to defect is low, while high ω helps sustain cooperation when temptation is high. The results are robust across weak and true prisoner's dilemma parameterizations. However, the paper does not study punishment, reward, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The findings may inform expectations about the impact of strategy space (continuous vs. binary) on cooperation, but do not address punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency. Thus, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited.

  findings: The study finds that the mean of reputation (interpreted as a proxy for cooperation) declines with increasing temptation (b), and is negatively affected by the proportion of elites and positively by the proportion of scoundrels. The diversity (standard deviation) of reputation increases with temptation and is affected in a quadratic manner by the proportion of elites and the local cooperation ratio. All results are based on simulation of a spatial prisoner's dilemma with three agent types and reputation updating.

- source: Capraro, V., Perc, M., & Vilone, D. (2020). Lying on networks: The role of structure and topology in promoting honesty. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that network structure has a nontrivial effect on the evolution of honesty and lying in the sender-receiver game. For black lies and altruistic white lies, honesty is more likely to evolve in small-world networks and, to a lesser extent, in the one-dimensional ring, compared to well-mixed populations. For Pareto white lies, lying is more likely to evolve in small-world networks. For spiteful lies, honesty always prevails regardless of network. The outcomes are reported in terms of the densities of liars and believers, not in terms of payoffs or efficiency. The sender-receiver game is fundamentally different from public goods games and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not study public goods games, does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency, welfare, or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on network structure and the evolution of honesty in the sender-receiver game are not transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in PGGs.
